- Reservoir sampling selects uniformly from a stream without storing the whole stream.
49. Q: How can a random node be selected from a complete binary search tree in O(log N) time and O(1) extra space?
A: Show answer
Assign each node a rank in the in-order sequence from 0 to N-1 by drawing one uniform random integer. At each node, determine the number of nodes in its left subtree from the complete-tree shape and current subtree size. If the rank equals the left-subtree count, select the current node; if it is smaller, descend left; if it is larger, subtract the left-subtree count plus one and descend right. Because the tree is complete, subtree sizes can be derived from its height and the number of nodes on the final level rather than stored in an auxiliary map. Each descent removes a level, giving O(log N) time and O(1) extra space when those counts are computed in constant time per level.

Study instruction:
- Work through a complete tree with seven nodes and map each possible rank to exactly one node.

Key point:
- Uniformly choosing a rank makes every node equally likely.
- Complete-tree shape information allows navigation without storing a separate node list.
50. Q: What is the difference between using randomization for shuffling, sampling, and load balancing?
A: Show answer
Shuffling seeks a uniformly random permutation of all positions. Sampling selects one or more members of a population with a specified probability, sometimes without replacement and sometimes while preserving stream order. Load balancing assigns incoming work to destinations so that capacity is used effectively; it may combine random candidate selection with measurements such as current load or response time. These tasks all use random choices, but their correctness criteria differ. A shuffle is judged by permutation uniformity, a sample by inclusion probabilities, and a load balancer by distribution, latency, throughput, and resilience.

Study instruction:
- For each task, state its outcome space and the property that a correct algorithm must preserve.

Key point:
- Randomization must be evaluated against the objective of the specific algorithm.
- Uniformity, representativeness, and operational efficiency are different requirements.
51. Q: What are the most important design checks before adopting a randomized algorithm?
A: Show answer
Define the objective and threat model first: determine whether the algorithm needs speed, approximate balance, unbiased sampling, reproducibility, or security. Identify the source of randomness and verify that its range and distribution match the algorithm's assumptions. Analyze whether different random-choice paths map evenly to valid outcomes, calculate expected and worst-case behavior, and consider state repetition or correlations. Test edge cases such as empty and singleton inputs, duplicate values, unavailable servers, and concurrent decisions. Finally, benchmark representative workloads and document whether the result is probabilistic or guaranteed by a deterministic invariant.

Study instruction:
- Apply this checklist to Fisher-Yates, randomized Quicksort, and two-choice load balancing and note which checks differ.

Key point:
- Correctness depends on both the random source and the structure of the algorithm using it.
- State assumptions, probabilistic guarantees, and failure handling explicitly.
52. Q: What are the main lessons about randomization and algorithm design?
A: Show answer
Randomization can make algorithms less sensitive to unfavorable input order, produce unbiased permutations when choices are structured correctly, reduce the risk of degenerate search trees, and distribute work effectively across destinations. Its success depends on more than calling a random-number function: the generator must suit the application, the probability of each outcome must be analyzed, and implementation details must preserve the intended distribution. Fisher-Yates demonstrates a mathematically uniform shuffle; naïve swapping demonstrates hidden bias; randomized Quicksort and tree construction demonstrate probabilistic protection against bad order; and the power of two choices demonstrates how limited random sampling plus local information can improve load distribution.

Study instruction:
- Summarize one benefit, one limitation, and one correctness condition for each major randomized technique in the chapter.

Key point:
- Randomization is powerful when its probability structure is understood and tested.
- Use a security-appropriate generator and never confuse expected performance with an absolute guarantee.
Rows: 52
Table 4
Cache Design, Eviction Policies, and Memory Locality
1. Q: What is caching, and why can it make software faster?
A: Show answer
Caching stores a copy of data obtained from a slower or more distant source in a location that can be accessed more quickly. When the same information is requested again, the application can retrieve the local copy instead of repeating the expensive external operation. For example, a product-search application can retain a product price after the first web search and reuse it for later requests. The benefit is greatest when data is requested repeatedly and does not change so quickly that the saved copy becomes unreliable.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between the original data source and the faster local copy, then identify an application in which repeated requests make caching useful."

Key point:
- A cache reduces the cost of repeated data retrieval by keeping a reusable local copy.
- Caching improves speed only when the saved value is sufficiently useful and current.
2. Q: Why does the distance between a computer and a data source affect retrieval time?
A: Show answer
Retrieval time is influenced by communication distance, network latency, intermediate systems, storage technology, and the work required to obtain the data. A local memory lookup is generally much faster than requesting information from a remote server because a remote request must travel through a network and wait for the server to respond. The chapter uses the Internet as an example of a distant source. Caching moves frequently needed information closer to the application so that many later requests avoid that remote delay.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a local lookup with a remote request and list the stages that can add delay to the remote operation."

Key point:
- Remote retrieval usually has a higher latency than local retrieval.
- Caching reduces repeated dependence on a slower source.
3. Q: What is the difference between a cache hit and a cache miss?
A: Show answer
A cache hit occurs when the requested item is already stored in the cache, allowing the application to use the cached value. A cache miss occurs when the item is absent, so the application must obtain it from the external or slower source. In a typical read-through sequence, the application checks the cache first, fetches the item after a miss, and then stores the fetched result for future use. Hits avoid the external retrieval cost; misses incur that cost and may also trigger an eviction when capacity is full.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a sequence of repeated requests and label each request as a hit or miss, explaining what the application does next."

Key point:
- A hit is served from the cache; a miss requires another source to be consulted.
- A miss may also require space management before the new value is stored.
4. Q: How does a simple hash-table cache work?
A: Show answer
A hash table can map a request key, such as a product name, to its cached result. The application first tests whether the key exists. If it does, the value is returned directly; if not, the application calls the external data source, stores the result under that key, and returns it. Hash-table lookup and insertion are expected O(1) operations, making a hash table a natural starting point for a cache. This simple design does not by itself solve capacity limits, expiration, or invalidation.

Study instruction:
- "Write out the hit and miss paths for a key-value cache and identify the point at which the external fetch occurs."

Key point:
- A hash table provides expected constant-time key-based access.
- A basic hash table cache still needs a policy for capacity and freshness.
5. Q: Why can an unbounded cache become a problem?
A: Show answer
Every cached entry consumes memory. If an application continually adds new items without removing old ones, the cache can grow until it competes with the rest of the application for available memory or causes memory exhaustion. A cache is intended to improve performance, not to retain every item ever encountered. A practical design therefore establishes a capacity limit or another resource budget and decides which entries should be removed when that limit is reached. Capacity should reflect both workload needs and the memory available to the application.

Study instruction:
- "Estimate how the number and size of cached entries affect memory use, and propose a reasonable capacity limit for a sample workload."

Key point:
- Caching trades memory for faster access.
- A useful cache must operate within a deliberate resource budget.
6. Q: What does it mean to evict an item from a cache?
A: Show answer
To evict an item is to remove it from the cache so that its storage can be reused. Eviction is normally triggered when a new item must be cached but the cache has reached its capacity. The application may still be able to retrieve the evicted item from the original data source later; eviction does not necessarily delete the authoritative record. The eviction policy determines which cached entry is removed, and its quality influences how often future requests become cache misses.

Study instruction:
- "Distinguish removal from a temporary cache from deletion of the authoritative data, using a product-price example."

Key point:
- Eviction frees cache capacity rather than necessarily deleting the original data.
- The choice of victim affects future hit and miss behavior.
7. Q: What is an eviction policy, and what is it trying to optimize?
A: Show answer
An eviction policy is the rule or algorithm used to choose which cached item to remove when space is needed. Its central objective is to retain items likely to be requested again soon while removing items whose future value is lower. A good policy tends to increase cache hits and reduce cache misses, thereby reducing the amount of slow external retrieval. The best policy depends on the request pattern, the cost of misses, the cache size, and whether the future request sequence is known or must be estimated.

Study instruction:
- "For a proposed workload, identify the information an eviction policy could use and explain how that information might predict later requests."

Key point:
- An eviction policy manages scarce cache capacity.
- Hit rate and miss cost are useful measures of policy effectiveness.
8. Q: What is a cold cache?
A: Show answer
A cold cache is a cache that has not yet been populated with the items needed by the current workload. Early requests therefore tend to be misses because the application has no stored results to reuse. As requests are served and their results are cached, the cache may become warmer and later requests may produce more hits. A cold start is not necessarily a design failure; it is an expected state after initialization, restart, or cache clearing. Performance evaluation should distinguish cold-start behavior from steady-state behavior.

Study instruction:
- "Describe what happens to hit rate during the first requests after an empty cache is initialized."

Key point:
- An empty or newly initialized cache begins cold.
- Early misses may be expected while useful entries are being populated.
9. Q: How can cache hit rate be calculated and interpreted?
A: Show answer
Hit rate is the number of cache hits divided by the total number of cache requests, usually expressed as a percentage. If 800 of 1,000 requests are served from the cache, the hit rate is 80 percent and the miss rate is 20 percent. These figures describe frequency, not the full performance benefit: a small number of very expensive misses may dominate response time, while inexpensive misses may matter less. Measure hit rate alongside latency, throughput, memory use, and the cost of fetching missed items.

Study instruction:
- "Calculate hit rate and miss rate from a sample request log, then explain why hit rate alone may not describe total performance."

Key point:
- Hit rate equals hits divided by total cache requests.
- A high hit rate is useful but must be interpreted with miss cost and latency.
10. Q: What is the farthest-in-future, or clairvoyant, eviction policy?
A: Show answer
The farthest-in-future policy evicts the cached item whose next request is farthest away in the known future request sequence. If an item will never be requested again in that sequence, it is an especially strong candidate for eviction. The reasoning is that keeping an item needed much later occupies space that could serve items needed sooner. The policy is called clairvoyant because it requires knowledge of future requests, not merely the requests already observed.

Study instruction:
- "Given a short request sequence and a full cache, mark the next use of each cached item and select the one whose next use is farthest away."

Key point:
- The policy uses future knowledge to choose the eviction victim.
- An item with no future use in the known sequence can be evicted safely for that trace.
11. Q: Why is farthest-in-future considered an ideal benchmark for eviction policies?
A: Show answer
When the complete request sequence is known and the cache has a fixed capacity, evicting the item whose next use is farthest away avoids sacrificing near-term hits in favor of distant requests. Any item removed by the policy is less urgent than the other cached items under the known trace. This makes the policy an optimal offline reference for comparing practical strategies on that same sequence. It is a benchmark rather than a generally deployable online policy because real applications usually do not know the future.

Study instruction:
- "Explain why a policy with future knowledge can serve as a theoretical benchmark even when a live application cannot implement it."

Key point:
- Clairvoyant eviction provides an optimal offline comparison for a fixed trace.
- Its advantage comes from information unavailable to an ordinary online cache.
12. Q: Why is a clairvoyant eviction policy usually impractical in an interactive application?
A: Show answer
An interactive application receives requests as users decide what to do. It cannot reliably know which product, page, or record a user will request next or when that request will occur. Without the complete future sequence, the application cannot determine which cached item is truly farthest from its next use. Some batch jobs do have a predetermined sequence and can exploit this information, but a general-purpose application needs a policy based on observable history or other available signals.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a batch job with a known input sequence to an interactive service whose next request is chosen by a user."

Key point:
- Future requests are generally unknown in interactive workloads.
- Offline-optimal policies are not automatically usable online.
13. Q: How does least-recently-used eviction work?
A: Show answer
Least-recently-used, or LRU, removes the cached item that has gone the longest without being requested. Each successful access makes that item the most recently used. When the cache is full and a new item must be inserted, the item at the least-recently-used end is evicted. LRU uses recent history as a practical estimate of future demand: an item that has not been requested for a while may be less likely to be needed soon than one requested recently.

Study instruction:
- "Maintain an ordered list of items while processing a request sequence, moving each hit to the most-recently-used position."

Key point:
- LRU evicts the item with the oldest last-access time.
- Recently used entries are retained in preference to less-recently used entries.
14. Q: What assumption about request patterns makes LRU useful?
A: Show answer
LRU relies on temporal patterns in demand: recently requested items are often requested again soon. This is a heuristic, not a guarantee. It is useful when popularity persists for a period and then shifts, such as a user revisiting pages or a product becoming temporarily popular. Recent accesses provide evidence of current interest. If requests have no stable relationship to recent history, LRU may perform less well because recency is then a weak predictor of future demand.

Study instruction:
- "Identify a workload with short-term popularity trends and another workload where recent access is unlikely to predict the next request."

Key point:
- LRU is most useful when recent activity predicts near-future demand.
- Its predictions depend on workload behavior rather than certainty.
15. Q: What is an LRU cache, and what does the name actually describe?
A: Show answer
An LRU cache is a cache that uses least-recently-used eviction. The name describes the item selected for removal, not the item the cache prefers to store. The cache retains the more recently used entries and evicts the least recently used one when capacity is required. This distinction matters because the phrase can be misunderstood as meaning that the cache stores only LRU items. In practice, LRU is the policy that maintains a set of currently useful entries according to recency.

Study instruction:
- "Explain in one sentence what LRU means and identify which end of an ordered cache represents the eviction candidate."

Key point:
- LRU names the eviction rule, not a special category of stored values.
- The most recently used item is protected from immediate LRU eviction.
16. Q: How should recency be represented in an LRU cache?
A: Show answer
A common representation is an ordered sequence in which the most recently used item is at the head and the least recently used item is at the tail. A hit moves the accessed item to the head. A newly cached item is also inserted at the head. When the cache is full, the tail is removed. This representation makes the policy easy to understand because position directly communicates recency, but the chosen data structure must support the required operations efficiently.

Study instruction:
- "Draw a four-entry recency list and update it after a mixture of hits and misses."

Key point:
- Head represents most recently used; tail represents least recently used.
- The order must be updated after every successful access.
17. Q: Why is a hash table alone insufficient for a conventional LRU cache?
A: Show answer
A hash table provides fast key lookup but does not maintain entries in recency order. After a hit, an LRU cache must identify the accessed entry and promote it to the most-recently-used position. When the cache is full, it must also identify the least-recently-used entry. A plain hash table does not provide an ordered head and tail for these operations. Additional bookkeeping is therefore required to combine fast lookup with efficient recency management.

Study instruction:
- "List the operations required by LRU and mark which ones a plain hash table can and cannot perform directly."

Key point:
- Fast lookup does not automatically provide recency ordering.
- LRU needs a second mechanism for tracking order.
18. Q: Why is an array combined with a hash table still inefficient for a fully constant-time LRU cache?
A: Show answer
The hash table makes key lookup fast, but an array creates costly updates to the recency order. Inserting at the front requires shifting the other entries, and moving a hit item to the front can require both a linear search and many shifts. Deleting from the opposite end can be constant time, but the hit-update and front-insertion costs remain O(N). The combination improves lookup but does not make every LRU operation constant time.

Study instruction:
- "For an array-based recency list, identify the number of entries that may need to move when the first or last position changes."

Key point:
- Array insertion at the front and arbitrary relocation can require O(N) work.
- Fast hash lookup does not remove the array-ordering bottleneck.
19. Q: Why is a doubly linked list a better partner for a hash table in an LRU cache?
A: Show answer
A doubly linked list tracks both the head and tail and gives each node links to its previous and next neighbors. It can insert at the head, remove from the tail, and detach a known node from the middle using a fixed number of pointer updates. These operations are O(1) when the node is already known. A hash table supplies the missing fast key-to-node lookup. Together, they support both constant-time expected lookup and constant-time recency updates.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the operations available at both ends of an array, a singly linked list, and a doubly linked list."

Key point:
- The doubly linked list supports efficient updates at both ends and around a known node.
- The hash table makes the required node available without traversing the list.
20. Q: How does the hash-table-and-doubly-linked-list design achieve expected O(1) cache reads?
A: Show answer
The hash table maps each cache key directly to its corresponding linked-list node. On a request, the application looks up the key in the hash table. If the key exists, it has the node reference immediately and can return the cached payload without scanning the list. It then moves the known node to the head to record the access. The lookup is expected O(1), and moving a known node is O(1), so the complete hit path is expected O(1).

Study instruction:
- "Trace a cache hit from the request key through the dictionary entry to the node, then show the pointer updates that promote the node."

Key point:
- The dictionary provides direct access to the node.
- The linked list updates recency without a linear search.
21. Q: What information should an LRU cache node contain?
A: Show answer
A node needs enough information to identify the cached entry and return its payload, plus references to its neighboring nodes. A typical node therefore contains a key or identifying field, the cached data, a previous-node reference, and a next-node reference. The key is also needed when an evicted node is removed from the hash table. The exact payload structure is application-specific, but the dictionary and linked-list references must describe the same cache entry.

Study instruction:
- "Design a node structure for a product-price cache and identify which fields support lookup, return, eviction, and pointer navigation."

Key point:
- A node stores the entry data and two neighbor references.
- The key must remain available so the matching dictionary entry can be deleted.
22. Q: What is the purpose of storing linked-list nodes as hash-table values?
A: Show answer
If the hash table stores a reference to each linked-list node, the application can find the exact node for a key immediately. It does not need to search the linked list for a matching payload after a hit. The linked list still maintains the order through its neighbor pointers, while the hash table supplies direct addressing by key. The dictionary and list are therefore two views of the same entries rather than two independent copies of the full cache contents.

Study instruction:
- "Draw a dictionary entry pointing to a node that is also linked to its neighbors, and explain why both structures can refer to the same object."

Key point:
- The hash table stores node references, not a separate searchable copy of the payload.
- The list controls order while the dictionary controls direct access.
23. Q: How is a cache hit handled in the hash-table-and-doubly-linked-list design?
A: Show answer
The cache checks the hash table for the requested key. On a hit, it retrieves the associated node, detaches that node from its current position, reconnects its previous and next neighbors, and inserts it at the head. The node becomes the most recently used entry. The hash-table mapping normally remains unchanged because the key still refers to the same node. If the node is already the head, no movement is required.

Study instruction:
- "Practice moving a head, middle, and tail node to the head, checking the previous and next references after each case."

Key point:
- A hit promotes the accessed node to the head.
- The dictionary mapping normally remains stable during promotion.
24. Q: How is a cache miss handled when the LRU cache has available capacity?
A: Show answer
When the requested key is absent and the cache is not full, the application obtains the value from the external source. It creates a node containing the key and value, inserts that node at the head of the doubly linked list, and stores a mapping from the key to the new node in the hash table. The new entry is most recently used because it has just been requested. No eviction is needed while capacity remains available.

Study instruction:
- "Write the ordered steps for a miss in a cache that still has one free slot and verify that both structures are updated."

Key point:
- A new entry is inserted at the head and added to the dictionary.
- A miss does not require eviction if capacity is still available.
25. Q: How is a cache miss handled when the LRU cache is full?
A: Show answer
The cache first removes the tail node, which represents the least recently used entry. It uses the evicted node’s key to delete the corresponding dictionary mapping. It then creates the new node, inserts it at the head, and adds the new key-to-node mapping. The order is important: both structures must stop referring to the evicted entry before or as the replacement is installed. This keeps the cache within its capacity and preserves consistency between the dictionary and list.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a full-cache miss and check that the evicted key is absent from both the list and the dictionary afterward."

Key point:
- The LRU victim is the tail node.
- Eviction must remove the entry from both structures.
26. Q: Why can inserting at the head and evicting at the tail both be O(1) in a doubly linked list?
A: Show answer
A doubly linked list maintains direct references to its head and tail. Inserting a new node at the head requires linking the new node to the old head and updating the old head’s previous reference. Removing the tail requires updating the new tail’s next reference and changing the list’s tail reference. Neither operation requires traversing the list. The constant-time claim assumes the list is already tracking valid endpoints and that the required node is directly known.

Study instruction:
- "Write the pointer changes for head insertion and tail removal without using a traversal loop."

Key point:
- Direct head and tail references eliminate endpoint searches.
- Constant-time pointer updates depend on preserving list invariants.
27. Q: What pointer changes are required to move a middle node to the head?
A: Show answer
Suppose node C sits between nodes B and D, while A is the current head. First connect B directly to D by setting B’s next reference to D and D’s previous reference to B. Then connect C to the old head by setting C’s next reference to A and A’s previous reference to C. Set C’s previous reference to None and make C the new head. These fixed pointer updates detach C and reinsert it without shifting other nodes.

Study instruction:
- "Draw a four-node list and annotate every previous and next reference before and after moving an interior node to the head."

Key point:
- Detach the node by reconnecting its neighbors.
- Attach it before the old head and update the head reference.
28. Q: What special case must be handled when the node being moved is the tail?
A: Show answer
When the selected node is the tail, it has no next neighbor. The implementation must reconnect its previous node to the end of the list and update the tail reference before attaching the selected node at the head. Treating the tail as though it had a next node would cause an invalid reference operation. A robust method also handles the case where the node is already the head, in which case it can return immediately without changing any links.

Study instruction:
- "Test the move-to-head operation separately for a head node, a middle node, and the tail node."

Key point:
- The tail case must update the list’s tail reference.
- The head case should not detach and reinsert the node unnecessarily.
29. Q: What invariants should always hold between the hash table and the linked list?
A: Show answer
Every cached key should have exactly one dictionary entry and exactly one corresponding list node. The dictionary value should point to that node, and the node should contain the key needed to remove the dictionary entry during eviction. Every node in the list should be reachable through the head-to-tail links, and the head and tail references should identify the actual endpoints. The number of dictionary entries should equal the number of list nodes and should never exceed the configured capacity.

Study instruction:
- "Create a checklist for verifying dictionary-to-node correspondence, list connectivity, endpoint references, and capacity after each operation."

Key point:
- The dictionary and linked list must represent the same set of entries.
- Count, key, and pointer invariants prevent stale or orphaned cache entries.
30. Q: What are the expected time complexities of the conventional hash-table-and-doubly-linked-list LRU cache?
A: Show answer
A key lookup in the hash table is expected O(1). Inserting a known new node at the head, removing the tail, detaching a known node, and reconnecting its neighbors are all O(1). Consequently, reads, promotions, insertions, and evictions are expected O(1) under the usual hash-table assumptions. The hash table can have worse behavior in pathological collision cases, and dynamic resizing may occasionally require reorganization, so the practical claim is expected or amortized constant time rather than an unconditional worst-case guarantee.

Study instruction:
- "Build a complexity table for lookup, hit promotion, insertion, and eviction, and state the assumptions behind the hash-table bound."

Key point:
- The combined design makes normal LRU operations expected O(1).
- The hash-table complexity is expected or amortized, not guaranteed worst-case O(1).
31. Q: Why does a conventional LRU cache store a reference to a node rather than search the linked list on every hit?
A: Show answer
A linked list is efficient at changing links when a node is known, but it is not efficient at finding an arbitrary node by key. Searching from the head may inspect up to N nodes and therefore costs O(N). By storing the node reference in the hash table, the cache avoids this traversal. The list is used for ordering and pointer manipulation, not for key-based searching. This division of responsibilities is what removes the linear-search bottleneck.

Study instruction:
- "Explain which data structure answers the question “where is this key?” and which answers “which item is least recent?”"

Key point:
- The hash table finds the node; the linked list orders the nodes.
- A known node can be moved in constant time, but an unknown node cannot be found that way.
32. Q: What edge cases should a production doubly linked list handle during removal?
A: Show answer
A reusable list should correctly handle an empty list, a one-node list, removal of the head, removal of the tail, and removal of a middle node. Removing the only node must set both head and tail to None. Removing the head must clear the new head’s previous reference; removing the tail must clear the new tail’s next reference. It should also reject or safely handle a node that does not belong to the list. These cases are easy to overlook when a demonstration cache always evicts from a full multi-entry list.

Study instruction:
- "Write unit tests for empty, singleton, head, tail, and middle removal and verify both endpoint references after each test."

Key point:
- Singleton removal must clear both endpoints.
- List methods should define behavior for invalid nodes and empty structures.
33. Q: What problem occurs if a cache inserts a key that is already present?
A: Show answer
If a cache blindly creates a second node for an existing key, the dictionary may be changed to point to the new node while the old node remains in the linked list. The list can then contain duplicate or orphaned entries, and a later eviction may delete the dictionary mapping for the wrong node. A robust cache should detect an existing key and choose an explicit update policy: replace its payload and move its node to the head, or remove the old node before inserting a replacement.

Study instruction:
- "Test repeated insertion of the same key and verify that the cache still contains one node and one dictionary entry for that key."

Key point:
- A key should not silently create duplicate list nodes.
- Updates must keep the dictionary and recency list synchronized.
34. Q: Why should a cache distinguish a missing key from a cached falsey value?
A: Show answer
A value such as zero, False, an empty string, or None may be a legitimate cached result. If application code tests the returned value only by truthiness, it can mistake a valid cached value for a miss and repeat the external fetch. A safer interface uses a unique sentinel or a separate presence indicator so that “not found” is distinct from “found with a falsey payload.” This is an implementation concern in the chapter’s example, where the consumer tests the returned data as a Boolean.

Study instruction:
- "Design a cache-read result that distinguishes a miss from cached values of zero, False, an empty string, and None."

Key point:
- Truthiness is not a reliable test for cache membership.
- Use an explicit miss sentinel or presence flag.
35. Q: Does an LRU eviction policy prevent stale cached data?
A: Show answer
No. LRU manages which entries remain when capacity is limited; it does not determine whether an entry is still accurate. A value can be recently used and still be out of date if the underlying source changed. Freshness requires a separate strategy, such as explicit invalidation, a time-to-live limit, version checks, or refresh rules. A cache design must therefore answer two different questions: which entries should remain in memory, and under what conditions may a stored value still be trusted?

Study instruction:
- "For a frequently changing price or account balance, specify an eviction policy and a separate freshness rule."

Key point:
- LRU controls capacity, not data validity.
- Freshness and eviction must be designed as separate concerns.
36. Q: How can an application reduce the risk of serving stale cached data?
A: Show answer
The application can invalidate an entry when the authoritative value changes, assign a time-to-live after which the entry must be refreshed, or associate cached data with a source version. The right approach depends on how quickly the data changes and how costly an outdated response would be. A price display may tolerate a short refresh interval, while permission or financial data may require stricter validation. The application should document its freshness guarantees and ensure that cache behavior does not override the authoritative source.

Study instruction:
- "Choose an invalidation or expiration method for three types of data with different rates of change and different consequences of staleness."

Key point:
- Invalidation and expiration address correctness, not merely memory capacity.
- Freshness rules should reflect the risk of using outdated information.
37. Q: What can go wrong when multiple threads or processes access the same LRU cache?
A: Show answer
A cache hit or miss often updates both the dictionary and several linked-list pointers. If two workers perform these operations simultaneously without coordination, one may observe a partially updated structure or overwrite another worker’s changes. Possible results include broken links, duplicate entries, lost mappings, or incorrect eviction. A concurrent implementation needs an appropriate synchronization strategy, such as a lock around compound mutations or a design specifically built for concurrent access. The required level of coordination depends on the runtime and deployment architecture.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the multi-step operations that must be treated as one consistent change when several workers share a cache."

Key point:
- Dictionary and list updates are compound operations.
- Concurrency control is needed to preserve structural invariants.
38. Q: How should cache capacity be selected?
A: Show answer
Capacity should be based on the memory budget, the size of cached entries, the number of distinct items in active use, and the expected request pattern. A cache that is too small may evict useful entries repeatedly; one that is too large may waste memory or increase pressure on the rest of the application. Capacity should be measured against observed workloads rather than selected solely by intuition. Monitor hit rate, miss latency, memory consumption, eviction frequency, and the effects of changing the limit.

Study instruction:
- "Propose a capacity for a sample workload and state which measurements would justify increasing or decreasing it."

Key point:
- Capacity balances memory cost against avoided retrieval cost.
- Workload measurements should guide capacity tuning.
39. Q: What is the LRU trap described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The LRU trap occurs when a repeating request cycle contains slightly more distinct items than the cache can hold. For example, if a cache holds four entries and the application repeatedly requests five items in the same order, the fifth item can evict the first. When the next cycle begins, the first item is missing and its insertion evicts the second; the second is then missing and evicts the third. The cache can end up missing on every request even though the workload repeats predictably.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a cache of four entries receiving a repeating sequence of five distinct keys and record the victim after every miss."

Key point:
- A cyclic working set just larger than capacity can defeat LRU.
- A repeating workload does not guarantee a high hit rate.
40. Q: Why can LRU perform badly when the working set is only slightly larger than the cache?
A: Show answer
When the active working set exceeds capacity by even one item and requests repeat in a fixed cycle, LRU can continually evict the next item that the application is about to request. Each new miss changes the recency order so that the following required item becomes the least recently used victim. The problem is not that the cache has no useful data at all; it is that the eviction order conflicts with the cycle. This is a workload-specific pathological case rather than evidence that LRU is always ineffective.

Study instruction:
- "Vary the cache size and cycle length in a small trace to identify when the repeating sequence begins to thrash."

Key point:
- A small capacity mismatch can create repeated eviction and refetching.
- The workload shape matters as much as the policy name.
41. Q: What is random replacement, and how does it differ from LRU?
A: Show answer
Random replacement selects a cache entry for eviction without using its recency or predicted future value. It is simple and avoids the particular deterministic cycle that creates the LRU trap, because it does not continually choose the least recently used item. However, it may evict an item that is about to be requested again and retain one that is no longer useful. It therefore gives up much of LRU’s predictive value in exchange for avoiding a fixed recency-based choice.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the information used by LRU and random replacement and describe one strength and one weakness of each."

Key point:
- Random replacement chooses a victim without recency information.
- Avoiding LRU’s trap does not make random replacement optimal.
42. Q: How does the power-of-two-choices eviction policy combine randomization with LRU?
A: Show answer
The policy randomly selects two cached entries and compares their recency. It evicts the less recently used of those two, rather than always evicting the globally least recently used entry. Random selection makes the victim less predictable and can avoid repeatedly choosing the same LRU position in a pathological cycle. Recency still contributes useful information because the policy retains the more recently used of the sampled pair. The method is a compromise between pure LRU and completely random replacement.

Study instruction:
- "For several pairs of randomly selected entries, identify which one is less recent and show which entry the policy removes."

Key point:
- Two random candidates are compared using recency.
- The policy preserves some LRU guidance while introducing randomness.
43. Q: Why might power-of-two choices avoid the LRU trap more effectively than pure random replacement?
A: Show answer
Pure random replacement ignores all evidence about recent use. Power-of-two choices still prefers the more recently used candidate, so it retains some ability to protect items that appear to be currently popular. At the same time, it does not deterministically evict the global LRU item on every miss, which can break the fixed sequence of victims responsible for the LRU trap. It is not guaranteed to outperform LRU on every workload; it is a practical heuristic intended to balance prediction and resistance to pathological recency patterns.

Study instruction:
- "Explain how the policy uses two different signals—random sampling and recency—and identify why neither signal alone is sufficient for every workload."

Key point:
- Randomization disrupts deterministic victim selection.
- Recency helps make the random choice more informed.
44. Q: How does the chapter implement power-of-two choices using linked-list indexes?
A: Show answer
The example generates two random indexes within the cache, chooses the larger index, traverses the linked list to that position, and removes the selected node. Because the head is the most recently used end and the tail is the least recently used end, a larger index represents a less recently used position. The selected node’s key is then deleted from the hash table. The method assumes the list has the configured full capacity when eviction is called.

Study instruction:
- "Trace the index selection for a four-node list and verify why the greater index represents the less recent of the two sampled positions."

Key point:
- A larger list index is closer to the LRU tail.
- The dictionary entry for the removed node must also be deleted.
45. Q: Why is removing a randomly selected linked-list node slower than removing the tail?
A: Show answer
The tail can be removed in O(1) because the doubly linked list already stores a direct tail reference. To remove an arbitrary randomly selected node by index, the implementation must first traverse from an endpoint until it reaches that position. The traversal can inspect O(N) nodes, even though unlinking the node itself takes only O(1) pointer changes once the node is known. This distinction explains why the randomized policy may avoid a workload problem while making eviction slower.

Study instruction:
- "Separate the cost of locating a node by index from the cost of unlinking a node whose reference is already available."

Key point:
- Searching for a random index is O(N) in a linked list.
- Unlinking a known node remains O(1).
46. Q: What assumptions in the power-of-two-choices example should be checked before reusing the code?
A: Show answer
The example draws indexes from zero through max_size minus one, so the list must actually contain max_size nodes whenever eviction runs. A more defensive implementation would use the current list length and validate that it is nonzero. The random draws may select the same index twice because they are made independently; that still produces a defined candidate but gives no meaningful comparison between distinct nodes. The pop-index method should also reject invalid indexes and correctly update head and tail references for endpoint cases.

Study instruction:
- "Review the random index bounds, duplicate sampling possibility, empty-list behavior, and list-length invariant in the eviction method."

Key point:
- Eviction bounds must match the current number of nodes.
- Random selection does not remove the need for defensive input and list checks.
47. Q: What is the memory hierarchy in a computer?
A: Show answer
The memory hierarchy is the arrangement of storage and memory levels with different capacities, costs, and access speeds. Small, expensive, fast levels sit near the processor, while larger, less expensive, slower levels hold data that is not needed as immediately. The design works because a program usually accesses only a subset of its total data at a given moment. Frequently needed information can be brought into faster levels, while the rest remains in larger, slower storage.

Study instruction:
- "Draw a memory hierarchy pyramid and label each level by relative speed, capacity, and cost per unit of storage."

Key point:
- Fast memory is generally smaller and more expensive.
- The hierarchy places actively needed data closer to the processor.
48. Q: Why do computers use several memory levels instead of making all memory equally fast?
A: Show answer
The fastest memory technologies are expensive and are not economical to use for all storage. A computer can provide a large total capacity at reasonable cost by combining small high-speed levels with larger slower levels. Most programs do not need every file and every data item at once, so a relatively small fast working area can serve the current computation. This hierarchy balances performance, capacity, power, and cost more effectively than a single uniform memory technology.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the trade-off between speed, capacity, and cost and why a layered design is economically practical."

Key point:
- A single all-fast memory system would be costly.
- A hierarchy provides fast access to a limited active working set.
49. Q: How do external data, filesystem storage, and RAM differ?
A: Show answer
External data, such as information on a remote web server, must be obtained through a network. Filesystem storage keeps persistent files on a local storage device. RAM holds the data and program state that the processor is actively working with. Applications commonly read a file from storage into RAM before processing it, then write changes back to persistent storage when needed. These levels differ in latency, capacity, persistence, and how software accesses them.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a file-opening operation from a remote source or local disk into RAM and identify where persistent and temporary copies exist."

Key point:
- Filesystem storage is persistent; RAM is the active working memory.
- Remote retrieval adds network delay beyond local storage access.
50. Q: Why does having a large disk not mean that a program can process an equally large data set in RAM?
A: Show answer
Disk capacity and RAM capacity serve different purposes. A computer may have terabytes of persistent storage but only a smaller amount of main memory. A program that loads a complete data set into RAM is limited by the available memory, which is also shared with the operating system and other applications. If the working data exceeds available RAM, the program may fail, slow down, or require a different processing strategy such as streaming or chunked processing.

Study instruction:
- "Given disk and RAM capacities, estimate whether a proposed in-memory data structure can fit after allowing for other running applications."

Key point:
- Disk capacity does not determine the amount of data that fits in RAM.
- Memory planning must account for the entire active working set.
51. Q: What roles do L3, L2, and L1 caches play in the memory hierarchy?
A: Show answer
Processor cache levels hold selected data from slower levels so the processor can access it more quickly. A typical hierarchy includes L3, L2, and L1, with L1 generally closest to the processor core and fastest but smallest, and lower levels generally larger and slower. Data may be brought from RAM into a larger cache and portions of it may also be available in smaller, faster caches. Exact sizes, sharing arrangements, and behavior vary by processor design.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the relative size, speed, and position of L1, L2, and L3 without assuming fixed capacities across machines."

Key point:
- Processor caches form multiple levels between RAM and execution.
- Hardware details vary by processor model.
52. Q: Why can CPU registers be described as an extremely fast storage level?
A: Show answer
CPU registers are small storage locations used directly by the processor while executing instructions. They hold operands, addresses, and intermediate values needed immediately by the current computation. Their proximity to execution hardware makes them faster to access than ordinary memory, but their capacity is extremely limited. The chapter informally describes registers as an L0-like cache to emphasize their speed and small size; this is a useful analogy rather than a universal formal cache-level naming convention.

Study instruction:
- "Explain why registers are fast and limited, and distinguish the chapter’s L0 analogy from formal processor cache labels."

Key point:
- Registers provide very fast storage for immediate processor operations.
- Their capacity is much smaller than RAM or processor caches.
53. Q: How do hardware caches help when a program iterates over an array?
A: Show answer
When a program accesses one element, the processor commonly brings a block of nearby memory into a cache rather than fetching only that single element. If the program next accesses neighboring elements, those values may already be in the cache. Sequential iteration can therefore reuse data that was fetched together and reduce slower memory accesses. The benefit depends on the actual memory layout and access pattern, but it explains why contiguous traversal is often faster than repeatedly jumping among distant locations.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a sequential array traversal and mark which later accesses may benefit from a block fetched for an earlier access."

Key point:
- Hardware often fetches nearby data together.
- Sequential access can reuse data already brought into cache.
54. Q: What is spatial locality, or locality of reference?
A: Show answer
Spatial locality is the tendency for a program that accesses one memory location to access nearby locations soon afterward. Hardware can exploit this tendency by loading a block containing adjacent data into a cache. Programs that traverse neighboring elements therefore have a greater chance of finding the next required data already available. Spatial locality is about physical or logical proximity of accessed data, not simply whether the same value is requested repeatedly.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the next memory locations accessed by two algorithms and decide which one is more likely to benefit from spatial locality."

Key point:
- Spatial locality favors accesses to nearby data.
- Memory layout and traversal order jointly influence locality.
55. Q: Why is iterating over rows usually faster than iterating over columns in a row-oriented two-dimensional array?
A: Show answer
In a row-oriented representation, the elements of each inner row are stored together or referenced in sequence. A row-first traversal processes neighboring elements before moving to another row, allowing fetched data to be reused. A column-first traversal jumps from one row to another for each element, so the next value may be far from the previous one and may not be in the same cache block. The chapter’s example shows that changing loop order alone can materially affect elapsed time even though both versions perform the same additions.

Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a two-dimensional traversal with the row index in the outer loop and compare the access sequence with a column-first version."

Key point:
- Traversal order should follow the underlying data layout.
- Row-first access improves locality in a row-oriented representation.
56. Q: Why can two algorithms with the same Big O complexity run at different speeds?
A: Show answer
Big O describes how an operation count or resource requirement grows with input size; it does not capture every hardware effect or constant factor. Two algorithms may perform the same asymptotic number of operations but access memory differently, allocate different amounts of data, or trigger different cache behavior. One may repeatedly use nearby memory while another jumps among locations. These differences affect actual elapsed time, so complexity analysis should be combined with attention to data layout, locality, allocation, and measurement.

Study instruction:
- "Choose two implementations with the same asymptotic complexity and list the hardware or memory-access factors that could make one faster."

Key point:
- Big O does not fully predict wall-clock performance.
- Memory access patterns can create large practical differences.
57. Q: How does column-first traversal reduce cache effectiveness in a row-oriented array?
A: Show answer
A column-first traversal accesses one element from the first row, then an element at the same column in the next row, and so on. If the rows are stored separately or far apart, each jump may require a different memory block. The cache may have loaded neighboring values from the first row, but the program does not use them before moving elsewhere. The row-first version consumes those neighboring values immediately. The problem is not the mathematical calculation but the mismatch between access order and memory layout.

Study instruction:
- "For a small matrix, write the exact sequence of coordinates visited by both loop orders and compare which sequence uses adjacent elements."

Key point:
- Column-first traversal may repeatedly miss the nearby data already fetched.
- The same computation can have different locality because of loop order.
58. Q: Does spatial locality guarantee a particular speed improvement?
A: Show answer
No. Spatial locality makes cache reuse more likely, but the actual benefit depends on the processor, cache sizes, memory layout, compiler or interpreter behavior, data types, and competing activity. The chapter reports one benchmark in which row-first traversal was about twice as fast as column-first traversal for a particular large array. That result illustrates the potential impact; it is not a universal ratio. A developer should measure representative workloads on the target environment rather than assume a fixed speedup.

Study instruction:
- "Treat the chapter’s benchmark as an illustration and identify at least three reasons another computer or workload may produce a different result."

Key point:
- Locality can improve performance, but the amount is system-dependent.
- Benchmark results should not be generalized without testing.
59. Q: Why is iterating over an array generally faster than iterating over a linked list, even though both take O(N) time?
A: Show answer
An array typically stores its element references in adjacent memory locations, so sequential traversal can benefit from cache lines and prefetching. Linked-list nodes may be allocated in unrelated locations, and each step depends on following a pointer to the next node. The processor may therefore wait for many separate memory accesses. Both traversals visit N elements and are O(N), but their constant factors and cache behavior can differ substantially. The exact result depends on the implementation and memory allocator.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the memory access sequence for array indexing and linked-list pointer traversal, not just the number of visited elements."

Key point:
- Arrays often provide better spatial locality than linked lists.
- Equal asymptotic complexity does not imply equal execution time.
60. Q: When might a linked list still be useful despite slower sequential traversal?
A: Show answer
A linked list can be useful when the application already has a reference to the node being changed and needs frequent insertions or removals without shifting a large array. In an LRU cache, a doubly linked list is valuable because the hash table supplies the node reference and pointer changes can move or remove it in constant time. The choice should therefore consider the dominant operations. If traversal speed is the main concern, an array may be preferable; if known-node relinking is central, a linked list may be justified.

Study instruction:
- "For a workload involving frequent searches, insertions, deletions, and full traversal, rank the operations by importance before selecting a structure."

Key point:
- Data-structure choice depends on the operations that dominate the workload.
- Linked lists trade locality for efficient relinking of known nodes.
61. Q: How can cache locality help explain why Quicksort is often faster than Mergesort in practice?
A: Show answer
Both Quicksort and Mergesort have O(N log N) time complexity in their typical comparison-based analysis, but their memory behavior differs. Quicksort commonly partitions and rearranges elements within the same array, repeatedly working in a relatively compact region. Mergesort commonly creates or uses additional arrays and moves data among them. Reusing the same array can improve cache locality, while juggling multiple arrays may create more memory traffic. This is one possible practical explanation, not the only reason Quicksort may be faster.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the memory locations each algorithm reads and writes during a representative sorting pass."

Key point:
- Quicksort’s in-place partitioning can improve reuse of the same array.
- Mergesort’s additional arrays can increase memory traffic.
62. Q: Does better cache locality make Quicksort universally faster than Mergesort?
A: Show answer
No. Cache locality is one factor among many, and performance depends on input distribution, pivot selection, implementation, memory allocation, stability requirements, recursion behavior, and hardware. Mergesort offers predictable O(N log N) time and stable sorting in common implementations, while Quicksort can have a poor worst case without suitable pivot handling. Some systems or data types may favor Mergesort or other algorithms. The chapter presents locality as an additional explanation for a common observed tendency, not as a universal ranking.

Study instruction:
- "List the practical requirements—stability, worst-case guarantees, memory budget, and data layout—that could change the sorting choice."

Key point:
- Cache locality is not the only determinant of sorting performance.
- Algorithm choice must reflect correctness and workload requirements as well as speed.
63. Q: How should developers benchmark cache-friendly code?
A: Show answer
Benchmark both implementations using the same input data, data size, runtime environment, and measurement method. Run multiple trials, separate setup costs from the timed section, and consider both cold and warmed-up behavior where relevant. Use representative inputs rather than a single unusually favorable case. Record elapsed time and, where possible, memory use and cache-related counters. The chapter’s row-versus-column timing is an example of how access order can matter, but measured results should be treated as evidence for the tested environment rather than a universal rule.

Study instruction:
- "Design a repeatable benchmark for row-first and column-first traversal, specifying input size, trial count, warm-up, and what will be timed."

Key point:
- A fair benchmark controls the input and measurement conditions.
- Repeat measurements and interpret results in their tested context.
64. Q: What are the main trade-offs when designing a software cache?
A: Show answer
A cache trades memory consumption and management complexity for faster access to repeated data. Designers must choose a capacity, an eviction policy, a freshness strategy, and a data structure that supports the required operations. They must also consider the cost of a miss, the workload’s repetition pattern, concurrency, and the consequences of serving outdated values. A more sophisticated cache may improve hit rate but use more memory or CPU time to maintain metadata. The best design is the simplest one that meets measured performance and correctness requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a cache design checklist covering capacity, eviction, freshness, key design, metrics, concurrency, and failure behavior."

Key point:
- Cache performance and cache correctness are separate design concerns.
- Policy complexity should be justified by workload measurements.
65. Q: How can a cache key be designed to avoid returning the wrong result?
A: Show answer
A cache key must distinguish every request dimension that can change the result. If a product price depends on region, currency, customer category, or promotion date, a key containing only the product name may return a result calculated for a different context. Keys should be normalized consistently and include the relevant parameters. They should also avoid accidental collisions between semantically different requests. Incorrect key design can create a high hit rate while returning invalid data, so key correctness is more important than maximizing hits.

Study instruction:
- "List all input parameters that affect a sample query and construct a key that uniquely represents the request context."

Key point:
- A cache key must represent the complete identity of the requested result.
- A hit is useful only if it returns data for the correct request context.
66. Q: What happens if a cache miss is followed by repeated simultaneous requests for the same key?
A: Show answer
If several workers observe the same key as missing, they may all fetch the same value from the external source before any one of them stores it. This duplicate work is sometimes called a cache stampede or duplicate-fill problem. It can overload the source and waste resources. A cache can reduce this risk by coordinating in-flight requests per key, allowing one worker to fetch while others wait for the result. The coordination mechanism must handle failures so waiting workers do not remain blocked indefinitely.

Study instruction:
- "Draw the timeline of two workers requesting the same absent key and identify where duplicate external calls occur."

Key point:
- Concurrent misses can cause duplicate fetches for one key.
- Per-key coordination can reduce redundant work.
67. Q: How can the chapter’s cache-miss exercise be solved using the clairvoyant policy?
A: Show answer
For the sequence c, t, h, o, p, t, h, z, o, a, p, t, b, z, h with capacity five, the first five distinct requests fill the cache and produce five misses. The requests for t and h are hits. On z, evict c because it has no later use in the remaining sequence. The request for o is a hit. On a, evict o because it is no longer needed in the remaining trace. The requests for p and t are hits. On b, evict any one of the cached items that will not be requested again before the trace ends. The final z and h are hits. Total: eight misses.

Study instruction:
- "Recreate the cache state after each miss and verify the next-use positions before choosing each victim."

Key point:
- The clairvoyant policy produces eight misses for this sequence and capacity.
- When several cached items have no future use, any of them can be selected.
68. Q: How can the same cache-miss exercise be solved using LRU?
A: Show answer
With capacity five, the first five requests c, t, h, o, and p are misses. The next t and h are hits and become the two most recent entries. The request for z is a miss and evicts c. The request for o is a hit. The request for a is a miss and evicts p; the later p miss evicts t; the later t miss evicts h; b then evicts z; z then evicts o; and the final h evicts a. The total is twelve misses. This is worse than the clairvoyant result because LRU cannot use future knowledge.

Study instruction:
- "Maintain an explicit most-recent-to-least-recent order after every request and count every miss, including initial fills."

Key point:
- LRU produces twelve misses for the stated sequence and capacity.
- The gap from eight clairvoyant misses illustrates the value of future knowledge.
69. Q: Which Bit Box counting method has better spatial locality, and what error must be corrected first?
A: Show answer
The method that completes one array before moving to the next has better spatial locality: it scans red bits sequentially, then blue bits, then green bits. The interleaved method jumps among three separate arrays for each index. However, the chapter’s class creates arrays of 10,000 elements while both methods loop 10,000,000 times. As written, both methods eventually raise an index error. The loop bounds must match the actual array lengths, or the arrays must be created with the intended larger size, before the locality comparison can be run.

Study instruction:
- "Correct the loop bounds or array sizes, then compare sequential-per-array traversal with interleaved traversal using the same valid element count."

Key point:
- Sequentially scanning each array offers better spatial locality in this example.
- The stated loop bound exceeds the arrays’ declared length and must be fixed.
70. Q: How can a data structure support expected O(1) search, O(1) insertion, and O(1) random sampling?
A: Show answer
Combine a dynamic array with a hash table. Store each distinct value in the array and map that value to its array index in the hash table. Search uses the hash table for expected O(1) membership and index lookup. Insertion appends the value to the array and records its index in the table, which is amortized O(1). Random sampling chooses a uniformly random valid array index and returns the value at that position in O(1). Each stored value has an equal chance of selection when each array position represents one equally weighted item.

Study instruction:
- "Implement the array and value-to-index map, then test membership, insertion, and repeated uniform random sampling."

Key point:
- The array supports constant-time random index access.
- The hash table supports expected constant-time value lookup.
71. Q: Why does an array make uniform random sampling straightforward?
A: Show answer
An array provides direct access by integer index. If it contains N equally weighted entries, choose an integer uniformly from zero through N minus one and return the entry at that index. Every position has the same selection probability, namely one divided by N. A linked list would not provide the same constant-time operation because reaching a randomly selected position generally requires traversal. The array therefore supplies the direct random-access capability that the hash table alone does not provide.

Study instruction:
- "For an array of N values, explain why selecting a uniform index gives each stored position probability 1/N."

Key point:
- Uniform index selection gives each array position an equal chance.
- Direct indexing makes the sample operation O(1).
72. Q: How can the array-and-hash-table structure be extended to support O(1) deletion?
A: Show answer
To delete a value, find its index through the hash table, move the array’s final element into the deleted position, update the moved element’s index in the hash table, and remove the final array slot. Then delete the removed value’s mapping. This avoids shifting all later array elements. The operation is expected O(1), assuming hash-table operations are expected constant time. The array order changes, so this technique is appropriate when order is not part of the data structure’s contract.

Study instruction:
- "Work through deletion of a middle array element and update the array and index map after swapping in the final element."

Key point:
- Swap-with-last avoids O(N) shifting during deletion.
- The moved value’s hash-table index must be updated.
73. Q: What does equal probability mean when a random-sample structure contains duplicate values?
A: Show answer
The requirement must specify whether each stored occurrence or each distinct value should be equally likely. If the array stores duplicate occurrences, choosing a uniform array index gives each occurrence equal probability, so a value appearing several times has a proportionally higher chance of being returned. If distinct values must be equally likely regardless of frequency, the structure needs a different representation that samples among distinct keys. The data model must define this rule before the sampling algorithm can be judged correct.

Study instruction:
- "Compare occurrence-based sampling with distinct-value sampling using an array such as [A, A, B]."

Key point:
- Uniform array-index sampling weights occurrences equally.
- Uniform sampling among distinct values requires a distinct-key representation.
