Selecting 500 residents from one neighborhood is not representative of an entire city if neighborhoods differ in income, age, political preference, or other relevant characteristics. Random selection helps prevent deliberate selection bias, but it cannot repair a frame that excludes whole groups or a survey in which particular groups systematically refuse to respond. The analyst should document eligibility rules, the source of the sampling frame, selection method, contact attempts, nonresponse, exclusions, and any weighting or adjustment used. Representativeness is a property of the design and execution, not merely a label attached to a sample after collection.

Study instruction:
- Review a proposed survey sample and identify coverage gaps, convenience-selection risks, nonresponse risks, and any population groups that may be underrepresented.

Key point:
- Random selection does not automatically eliminate every form of bias.
- Define the population and assess the sampling frame before collecting observations.
9. Q: How should the size of a random sample be chosen?
A: Show answer
Sample size should be based on the precision required, the variability of the characteristic being estimated, the desired confidence level, the sampling design, and practical constraints such as cost and response rates. A sample of 500 may be adequate for one population estimate and inadequate for another. The population size matters, especially for small populations, but for very large populations the required sample size is often driven more by the desired margin of error and variability than by the total population count. Clustered or stratified designs can change the effective sample size, and expected nonresponse may require inviting more people than the number of completed responses ultimately needed. The sample size should therefore be justified before the results are examined; choosing it after seeing a favorable result can introduce selective reporting and overconfidence.

Study instruction:
- For a survey or quality-control exercise, document the target precision, confidence level, expected variability, sampling design, nonresponse allowance, and reason for the selected sample size.

Key point:
- There is no universally correct sample size such as 100 or 500.
- Precision, variability, design effects, and response rates determine the requirement.
10. Q: What is the difference between sampling error and sampling bias?
A: Show answer
Sampling error is the natural variation that occurs because a sample contains only some members of the population. Two properly selected random samples can produce slightly different estimates even when both are unbiased. Sampling bias is a systematic distortion caused by the selection process, coverage gaps, nonresponse, measurement practices, or other factors that make some outcomes more likely to be represented than others. Increasing the sample size generally reduces random sampling variability, but it does not necessarily remove systematic bias. For example, surveying more people through a website may still underrepresent residents who have limited internet access. A large biased sample can therefore produce a very precise estimate of the wrong quantity. Analysts should investigate both the variability of the estimate and the possibility that the design systematically excludes or mismeasures relevant groups.

Study instruction:
- Create one example of random sampling error and one example of selection bias, then explain why increasing the sample size solves only the first problem.

Key point:
- Sampling error is random variation; sampling bias is systematic distortion.
- A larger sample cannot automatically repair a flawed sampling process.
11. Q: When might stratified sampling be preferable to selecting one simple random sample?
A: Show answer
Stratified sampling is useful when the population contains important subgroups that should all be represented, such as geographic regions, age groups, customer categories, or product types. The population is divided into mutually exclusive strata, and observations are sampled from each stratum according to a defined allocation. Proportional allocation selects in line with each stratum's share of the population; disproportionate allocation may deliberately oversample smaller or analytically important groups, with appropriate weighting when producing an overall estimate. This can improve subgroup coverage and, when strata are internally more homogeneous than the population as a whole, may improve precision. The design requires reliable information for assigning population members to strata and careful handling of weights. Stratification does not mean selecting convenient participants from each group; the selection within each stratum must still follow the sampling plan.

Study instruction:
- Design a stratified sample for a population with at least three meaningful groups and explain how you would weight the results if the allocation is disproportionate.

Key point:
- Stratification protects representation of specified subgroups.
- Selection within each stratum must still be systematic and defensible.
12. Q: How can a sample mean be used to estimate the mean of a very large array?
A: Show answer
Select a sample of values using a suitable random design, add the sampled values, and divide by the number of observations in the sample. If the array contains one million values, a sample of 1,000 values requires approximately 1,000 value additions rather than one million, assuming the sample can be accessed without first scanning or rearranging the entire dataset. Under a suitable probability-sampling design, the sample mean is an estimator of the population mean; it is not guaranteed to equal the exact mean in any particular run. A different sample will usually produce a different estimate. The analyst should record the sample size, selection method, random seed where reproducibility is required, and an estimate of uncertainty. The sample mean is most useful when its expected error is small enough for the decision being supported.

Study instruction:
- Calculate the exact mean of a small dataset, draw several random samples from it, calculate each sample mean, and compare the estimates with the exact result.

Key point:
- The sample mean estimates the population mean at lower computational cost.
- The estimate varies between samples and should be accompanied by its sampling design and uncertainty.
13. Q: What is the difference between sampling with replacement and sampling without replacement?
A: Show answer
Sampling with replacement allows a selected item to be selected again in a later draw. Repeated random index selection, such as calling random.randint for every observation, can therefore include the same array element multiple times. Sampling without replacement prevents an item from appearing more than once in the sample. Both designs can be valid, but their probability calculations and variability differ. With replacement, draws are independent when the selection mechanism is correctly implemented, which can simplify some probability analyses. Without replacement, the draws are dependent because each selection changes the remaining pool, and the sample may contain more distinct population members for a fixed sample size. The analyst must identify which design is used and use an uncertainty calculation consistent with it rather than treating the two designs as interchangeable.

Study instruction:
- Run a small sampling experiment with and without replacement, record duplicate counts, and explain how the two selection designs affect the resulting sample.

Key point:
- Repeated random indices usually imply sampling with replacement.
- State the sampling design because it affects both the data and the uncertainty calculation.
14. Q: How can the reliability of a sample-mean estimate be assessed?
A: Show answer
The sample mean alone does not show how precisely the population mean has been estimated. Reliability depends on the sample size, the spread of the observations, the sampling design, and whether the observations can reasonably be treated as independent. For a simple random sample with suitable assumptions, the standard error of the mean is commonly estimated as the sample standard deviation divided by the square root of the sample size. A confidence interval can then express a range of values compatible with the data under the chosen statistical model. Larger samples generally reduce standard error, while highly variable data require more observations for the same precision. Clustering, heavy tails, extreme outliers, missing data, or selection bias may make a simple formula misleading, so diagnostics and design-specific methods are necessary.

Study instruction:
- Calculate a sample mean, sample standard deviation, and estimated standard error for a small dataset; list the assumptions that would need checking before interpreting a confidence interval.

Key point:
- An estimate should be evaluated together with its uncertainty and assumptions.
- Sample size alone does not determine precision when data variability and design differ.
15. Q: Why can shuffling an entire array before sampling undermine the intended speed advantage?
A: Show answer
Shuffling a million-element array requires work across the array and generally takes time proportional to the number of elements. If the objective is to inspect only a few hundred values, performing a full shuffle first can eliminate much of the computational saving that sampling was intended to achieve. The chapter's example uses a shuffled array to make the demonstration easy to understand, but a production design should examine the full data path rather than counting only the sample loop. If the data is already in memory and supports efficient random indexing, randomly selected indices may be sufficient. If the data is stored in a stream or cannot be randomly accessed, a method such as reservoir sampling can maintain a uniform sample in one pass with memory proportional to the sample size. The right method depends on how the data is stored and accessed.

Study instruction:
- Profile the cost of loading, shuffling, selecting, and processing a dataset separately; identify which operations actually dominate runtime and memory use.

Key point:
- Sampling is only cheaper if sample selection does not require processing the whole population first.
- Optimize the complete data-access workflow, not just the calculation loop.
16. Q: How can random sampling be used to approximate the median of a large dataset?
A: Show answer
Draw a random sample, sort only the sampled values, and select the middle value when the sample size is odd. If the sample size is even, the median is conventionally the mean of the two central ordered values. For example, a sample of 501 values has a middle position at index 250 when using zero-based indexing, so the sample median can be obtained after sorting those 501 observations. This avoids sorting the entire population when the sample can be selected efficiently. The work includes sample selection and sorting, so the sorting component is approximately proportional to k log k for a sample of size k, rather than N log N for the full dataset. The result remains an estimate: it may differ from the population median, especially when the sample is small or the distribution is irregular.

Study instruction:
- Use an odd-sized and an even-sized sample, sort each, and calculate the median correctly; compare both estimates with the median of the complete dataset.

Key point:
- The sample median is the middle order statistic of the sampled values.
- Only the sample is sorted, but the estimate still has sampling uncertainty.
17. Q: Why might a sample median be less stable than a sample mean in some datasets?
A: Show answer
The precision of a sample median depends on how observations are distributed around the population's 50th percentile. If many values are tightly concentrated near the median, small changes in which observations are sampled can move the estimated middle position noticeably. The sample mean uses information from every sampled value, whereas the median is determined by order and the central position, so the two estimators respond differently to the same data. However, the median is more resistant to extreme values than the mean, which can make it preferable for skewed distributions or data with outliers. There is no universal rule that a sample median is always less accurate. The appropriate comparison depends on the distribution, sample size, loss function, and whether the target is the mean or the median itself.

Study instruction:
- Create one symmetric dataset and one highly skewed dataset; compare the behavior of the sample mean and sample median under repeated sampling and added outliers.

Key point:
- Mean and median have different statistical properties.
- Evaluate the estimator against the target quantity and the shape of the data.
18. Q: When is a sample mean more appropriate than a sample median, and when might the reverse be true?
A: Show answer
Use a sample mean when the target is the arithmetic average and the distribution is sufficiently well behaved for the mean to be informative. The mean is sensitive to every observation, including unusually large or small values, which is useful when total magnitude matters but can make it unstable in heavily skewed data. Use a median when the typical central position is more meaningful or when extreme values would distort the mean. For example, average transaction value may be relevant to total financial exposure, while median transaction value may better describe the typical transaction in a dataset containing a few very large purchases. The choice should follow the question being answered, not whichever statistic produces the more attractive result. A sample estimate of either measure must still be accompanied by appropriate uncertainty analysis.

Study instruction:
- For income, transaction values, response times, and test scores, state whether the mean or median answers the decision question more clearly and explain why.

Key point:
- The mean measures an arithmetic average; the median measures the central ordered position.
- Choose the statistic that matches the purpose and distribution of the data.
19. Q: How can random sampling estimate the percentage of even and odd integers in a large array?
A: Show answer
Select a random sample of k array elements, count how many are even, and divide that count by k to estimate the even proportion. An integer is even when value % 2 == 0; this condition also works for zero and negative even integers. The estimated odd proportion is the number of sampled odd values divided by k, or one minus the estimated even proportion when every sampled value is classified into exactly one of the two groups. Multiply each proportion by 100 to express it as a percentage. If the sample contains 312 even values out of 500, the estimated even percentage is 62 percent and the estimated odd percentage is 38 percent. These are estimates, not exact population percentages, and the uncertainty depends on the sample size and selection design.

Study instruction:
- Implement the estimator on a large test array, compare it with the exact even and odd percentages, and repeat the experiment with several sample sizes.

Key point:
- Estimate a category percentage as its sample count divided by the sample size.
- The even and odd estimates should sum to 100 percent when every value is classified correctly.
20. Q: What is primality testing, and why is it important beyond elementary mathematics?
A: Show answer
Primality testing determines whether an integer greater than one is prime or composite. A prime number has exactly two positive divisors, one and itself; a composite number has at least one additional positive divisor. Prime numbers matter in number theory and are used in important cryptographic constructions, where the properties of large integers affect security. Testing a small number by trial division is straightforward, but the candidate integers used in cryptographic systems can contain many digits, making exhaustive trial division impractical. A primality test must therefore balance computational cost with the level of assurance required. A probable-prime result is not automatically a proof of primality, and a test suitable for an educational demonstration should not be assumed suitable for production cryptographic key generation.

Study instruction:
- Classify several integers as prime or composite and explain why the assurance required for a cryptographic application is higher than for a classroom exercise.

Key point:
- Primality testing is a computational decision about the divisors of an integer.
- Application context determines whether probable-prime evidence is sufficient.
21. Q: How does brute-force primality testing work, and what is its main limitation?
A: Show answer
The basic method tests whether the candidate integer n is divisible by any integer from 2 through n - 1. If any divisor produces a zero remainder, the candidate is composite and the function can stop immediately. If no divisor is found, the candidate is prime. This method is easy to understand and provides an exact result for valid inputs, but it may perform a very large number of divisions. A naive implementation that tests every integer has a running time proportional to n in the worst case, which becomes impractical as the numeric value grows. It also needs explicit handling for values below 2; otherwise a loop with no iterations could incorrectly classify 0, 1, or a negative integer as prime. Correctness and input validation must be considered separately from optimization.

Study instruction:
- Trace the divisor loop for a prime and a composite number, then test the function against negative integers, 0, 1, 2, and small composite values.

Key point:
- Trial division is exact but can require many operations.
- Handle the mathematical domain before relying on an empty loop to imply primality.
22. Q: Why can an optimized trial-division test skip even divisors after checking whether the candidate is 2?
A: Show answer
Every even integer greater than 2 is divisible by 2 and therefore composite. The algorithm can first return True when n == 2, then return False when n % 2 == 0. For an odd candidate greater than 2, no even divisor can divide it, so the remaining trial divisors only need to be odd numbers beginning at 3. Incrementing the divisor by 2 avoids half of the divisions that a loop over every integer would perform. This optimization does not change the answer because it removes only divisors that are mathematically incapable of being factors of an odd candidate. It is a constant-factor improvement, however, and does not change the square-root asymptotic bound that becomes important for larger inputs.

Study instruction:
- Modify a trial-division function to handle 2 separately and test only odd divisors; count the number of divisions saved for several odd candidates.

Key point:
- Check 2 as a special case, reject other even candidates, and test odd divisors only.
- Skipping even divisors reduces work without changing correctness.
23. Q: Why is it sufficient to test possible divisors only up to the square root of a candidate number?
A: Show answer
If a composite integer n can be written as n = a * b, at least one of the two factors must be less than or equal to the square root of n. If both factors were greater than the square root, their product would be greater than n, which is impossible. Therefore, if no integer divisor from 2 through the square root divides n, no larger divisor can form a factor pair that has not already been considered. For example, testing 37 only requires checking integer divisors up to its square root, which is a little more than 6; after that point, any factor would have a paired factor in the range already examined. The loop should include the square-root boundary, because a perfect square can have a divisor exactly equal to its square root.

Study instruction:
- Prove the factor-pair argument using three composite numbers, including one perfect square, and explain why the loop condition must include the boundary.

Key point:
- Every composite number has a factor no greater than its square root.
- Testing through the square-root boundary is sufficient for exact trial division.
24. Q: What does an O(sqrt(N)) primality-testing algorithm tell us, and why can it still be too slow?
A: Show answer
The notation describes how the number of trial-division iterations grows with the numeric value N, assuming each arithmetic operation is treated as constant cost. Testing only odd divisors up to the square root reduces the number of candidate divisors substantially compared with checking every integer below N. However, the square root of a very large integer can still be enormous. A candidate with hundreds of digits is far beyond the practical reach of a loop that attempts billions or vastly more divisions. Big O notation also hides the cost of arithmetic on large integers: division and remainder operations become more expensive as the bit length increases. The square-root method is a useful exact baseline and a teaching example, but it is not a practical general solution for cryptographic-size candidates.

Study instruction:
- Compare the approximate iteration counts for O(N) and O(sqrt(N)) at several numeric magnitudes, and explain why asymptotic improvement does not guarantee practical feasibility.

Key point:
- O(sqrt(N)) is much better than O(N) but still grows too quickly for huge candidates.
- Big O analysis should be supplemented by the cost of large-integer arithmetic.
25. Q: Why is randomly trying a fixed number of possible divisors a poor Monte Carlo primality test?
A: Show answer
A simple randomized approach might choose 100 integers and test whether any divides the candidate. If none divides it, the algorithm labels the candidate probably prime. The problem is that a composite number may have very few non-trivial factors, especially when it is the product of two large prime factors. Randomly selecting 100 divisors from a huge range is unlikely to hit either factor, so the algorithm can report “prime” for many composite candidates. The number of trials is bounded, but the probability of finding a factor may be far too small to provide useful assurance. A randomized method is not effective merely because it performs random trials; the mathematics must establish that a correct answer is likely under the sampling rule. The Fermat-based method improves the test by using a number-theoretic property rather than hoping to guess a factor directly.

Study instruction:
- Construct a composite number with two large factors and explain why a small set of random trial divisors is unlikely to discover either factor.

Key point:
- Random search is ineffective when the event being searched for is too rare.
- A sound Monte Carlo test needs a mathematical bound on its error behavior.
26. Q: What does Fermat's Little Theorem state in the form used for primality testing?
A: Show answer
If p is prime and a is an integer not divisible by p, then a raised to the power p - 1 leaves a remainder of 1 when divided by p. In modular notation, this is a^(p - 1) mod p = 1. For a prime candidate, every integer from 1 through p - 1 is relatively prime to p, so each of those bases satisfies the congruence. The theorem provides a quick relationship between exponentiation and divisibility that can be checked using modular arithmetic. It does not state that every composite number fails the congruence for every base, nor does it prove primality when a single base passes. The distinction between a necessary property of prime numbers and a sufficient proof of primality is central to using the theorem correctly.

Study instruction:
- Apply the formula to several bases for a small prime such as 7, and explain why the base must not be divisible by the prime modulus.

Key point:
- For prime p and gcd(a, p) = 1, a^(p - 1) mod p equals 1.
- The theorem supplies a one-way test, not a complete primality proof.
27. Q: Why does a Fermat-test result different from 1 prove that a candidate greater than 1 is composite?
A: Show answer
Fermat's Little Theorem guarantees that every prime candidate p will produce a remainder of 1 for every permitted base a. The contrapositive is that if a valid computation for a candidate n produces a remainder other than 1, n cannot be prime and must be composite. Such a base is called a Fermat witness to compositeness. This conclusion is one-sided: a failed test is decisive, while a passed test is only evidence consistent with primality. The arithmetic should be performed correctly, the modulus must be greater than 1, and the input must be in the intended domain. A program should not treat a successful modular exponentiation as a proof unless it uses a method with a genuine deterministic guarantee for the relevant input range.

Study instruction:
- For several composite candidates, find a base that produces a residue other than 1 and explain why that single result is conclusive.

Key point:
- One Fermat witness is enough to prove compositeness.
- Passing a Fermat test does not establish primality.
28. Q: How does Fermat's randomized primality test work step by step?
A: Show answer
For a candidate integer n greater than 1, select a base a from the permitted range and compute pow(a, n - 1, n). If the result is not 1, return False because the candidate is composite. If the result is 1, repeat with another randomly selected base. After the configured number of trials all return 1, return a probable-prime result rather than claiming a mathematical proof. The chapter's implementation performs 100 trials and chooses each base with random.randint(1, n - 1). That fixed trial count limits the number of modular exponentiation checks, while the quality of the conclusion depends on the candidate and the probability properties of the selected bases. A robust implementation must also validate small inputs and state clearly what a True result means.

Study instruction:
- Trace the function for one composite candidate that fails immediately and one candidate that passes every configured round; label the second result as probable prime.

Key point:
- A non-1 residue stops the test with a composite result.
- Passing every round yields probable-prime evidence, not certainty.
29. Q: What kind of error can Fermat's primality test make, and what kind can it rule out?
A: Show answer
A correctly implemented Fermat test can identify a composite number whenever a tested base produces a residue other than 1. It cannot make the opposite mistake of declaring a prime candidate composite, because every permitted base for a prime satisfies Fermat's congruence. Its possible error is a false positive: a composite candidate may pass all tested bases and be reported as probably prime. This is a one-sided error profile. It is important to distinguish “no witness was found” from “the number has been proved prime.” The result should be communicated with that limitation, especially when another system will act on it. A test that has one-sided error may be suitable for screening, but a downstream process may still need a stronger test or a proof before accepting the candidate.

Study instruction:
- Draw a two-column decision table showing the true status of a candidate and the possible Fermat-test outcome; identify which cell represents the possible error.

Key point:
- A Fermat witness conclusively identifies a composite number.
- A composite number can pass the tested bases, creating a false positive.
30. Q: Why do repeated independent Fermat tests reduce the chance of a false positive for many composite numbers?
A: Show answer
For a composite number that is not a Carmichael number, no more than half of the relatively prime bases satisfy the Fermat congruence. If bases are selected independently according to a suitable uniform rule, the probability that every trial happens to choose a passing base decreases multiplicatively. Under the simplified upper bound of one-half per independent trial, four consecutive passes have an upper-bound false-positive probability of 1/16, and 100 consecutive passes have an upper bound of 1/2^100. These are conditional bounds for the stated class of composites and sampling assumptions, not universal probabilities that a candidate is prime. Independence matters: reusing the same base, using a predictable sequence with correlated outcomes, or applying an incorrect base-selection rule invalidates the simple multiplication. Carmichael numbers are a significant exception to the usual Fermat error bound.

Study instruction:
- Calculate the conditional false-positive bound for 1, 4, 10, and 100 independent rounds, then list the assumptions needed for the calculation to be valid.

Key point:
- Repeated independent trials can reduce false-positive risk exponentially for non-Carmichael composites.
- The bound must not be applied without checking its mathematical and sampling assumptions.
31. Q: If three of four Fermat tests return 1 and one returns 2, what can be concluded about the candidate?
A: Show answer
The candidate is composite. The single result of 2 is a Fermat witness because the required remainder is 1 for every tested base when the candidate is prime. The other three passing results do not cancel or weaken that logical conclusion. This illustrates why the test has one-sided error: a single failing base is decisive, while any number of passing bases cannot by themselves establish primality. The number of trials is therefore not a voting system in which a majority determines the answer. The algorithm should stop as soon as a valid witness is found, record the witness and residue for auditability, and return a composite result. There is no need to continue spending computation on a candidate that has already been proved composite.

Study instruction:
- Work through the four outcomes in order and explain why the first non-1 result determines the final classification regardless of the other results.

Key point:
- One valid non-1 residue is conclusive evidence of compositeness.
- Fermat testing is not majority voting across trial outcomes.
32. Q: If all four Fermat tests return 1, does that mean the candidate has a 1-in-16 chance of being prime?
A: Show answer
No. Under the simplified assumption that a non-Carmichael composite has at most a one-half chance of passing each independent trial, four passes imply that the conditional probability of this particular kind of composite producing all four passes is at most 1/16. That is not the same as saying the candidate has a 15-in-16 probability of being prime, or a 1-in-16 probability of being prime. The probability that the candidate is prime after observing the results also depends on the prior prevalence of prime and composite candidates in the population being tested, the sampling design, and whether Carmichael numbers or other exceptions are possible. This is a Bayesian distinction between the probability of the evidence given a composite candidate and the probability of primality given the evidence. Without a prior model, the test result alone does not supply an exact posterior probability.

Study instruction:
- Rewrite the 1-in-16 statement in conditional-probability language and identify the prior information needed to calculate a posterior probability of primality.

Key point:
- A false-positive bound is not the same as the probability that a candidate is prime.
- Posterior confidence requires a prior model and valid likelihood assumptions.
33. Q: What are Carmichael numbers, and why do they weaken Fermat primality testing?
A: Show answer
A Carmichael number is a composite integer n for which a^(n - 1) mod n equals 1 for every base a that is relatively prime to n. Such numbers are composite but behave like primes under the Fermat congruence for all coprime bases. A Fermat test that selects only coprime bases can therefore fail to find a witness for a Carmichael number, regardless of how many such bases it tests. This defeats the usual one-half false-positive argument for non-Carmichael composites. The qualification about coprimality is essential: a base that shares a factor with a Carmichael number does not satisfy the congruence and can expose compositeness. Thus, a statement that every integer from 1 to n - 1 passes for a Carmichael number is too broad; the defining property applies to bases relatively prime to n.

Study instruction:
- State the Carmichael property with the coprimality condition and explain how a test that repeatedly samples only coprime bases can be fooled.

Key point:
- Carmichael numbers are composite Fermat pseudoprimes for every coprime base.
- The theorem's base conditions must be stated precisely.
34. Q: What does the Carmichael number 561 demonstrate about the limits of Fermat testing?
A: Show answer
The integer 561 is composite because it factors as 3 11 17, yet it is a Carmichael number. Every base relatively prime to 561 satisfies the Fermat congruence, so a test that samples only coprime bases will always pass it. However, not every integer from 1 through 560 is relatively prime to 561. There are φ(561) = 2 10 16 = 320 coprime bases, out of 560 possible bases in that range. If a program samples uniformly from all 560 values with replacement, the probability that one draw is a passing coprime base is 320/560 = 4/7; non-coprime bases can reveal compositeness. This corrects an over-broad reading of the chapter's simplified description that all bases pass for 561. The defining Carmichael property is restricted to coprime bases.

Study instruction:
- Factor 561, calculate its totient using its three distinct prime factors, and distinguish the set of coprime bases from all integers below 561.

Key point:
- 561 is composite but passes Fermat's congruence for every coprime base.
- Non-coprime bases are not covered by the Carmichael property and may expose a factor.
35. Q: How should Fermat-test bases be selected, and why does the choice matter?
A: Show answer
The base-selection rule determines which mathematical error bound is applicable. The chapter's code selects an integer from 1 through n - 1, allowing repeated draws and including base 1, which always produces a passing result and therefore provides no useful evidence. A practical implementation should define a valid range, handle small candidates separately, and avoid treating a passing trivial base as meaningful evidence. It can also test whether gcd(a, n) is greater than 1; if so, the common factor proves that n is composite. However, a program must not discard every non-coprime draw and silently resample until it gets a coprime base if it intends to use such draws to detect factors. For Carmichael numbers, restricting the test to coprime bases makes the Fermat weakness particularly severe. Document the base distribution and the assumptions behind any probability claim.

Study instruction:
- Compare base ranges that include 1, exclude trivial endpoints, and explicitly check the greatest common divisor; explain how each design affects evidence and probability claims.

Key point:
- The distribution of bases is part of the algorithm, not an incidental coding detail.
- Do not discard useful factor-revealing bases without understanding the effect on Carmichael numbers.
36. Q: Why is Python's three-argument pow preferable to calculating a huge exponent and then taking a remainder?
A: Show answer
The expression a raised to n - 1 and then reduced modulo n first constructs the potentially enormous integer produced by exponentiation and only then computes its remainder. For a large exponent, that intermediate integer can require excessive time and memory. Python's pow(a, n - 1, n) performs modular exponentiation, computing the result modulo n during the exponentiation process rather than materializing the full power. This is exactly the operation required by Fermat testing. The third argument is the modulus, so pow(base, exponent, modulus) returns the modular power and is mathematically equivalent to taking the remainder of the full power for the relevant integer inputs, while being much more efficient for large values. Using modular exponentiation is an implementation requirement for practical large-integer testing, not merely a stylistic preference.

Study instruction:
- Compare the mathematical expressions and test the three-argument pow form on small values where the full exponent can be computed safely; avoid constructing enormous intermediate powers in performance tests.

Key point:
- pow(a, b, m) computes modular exponentiation efficiently.
- Avoid building a huge integer only to discard most of it with a remainder operation.
37. Q: What input checks and implementation safeguards should be added to the chapter's Fermat-test function?
A: Show answer
First reject integers below 2, because they are not prime and the chapter's random range is invalid for some of them. Handle 2 and other small candidates explicitly so the base-selection range is valid. Reject even candidates greater than 2 before running randomized rounds. For each round, select a base from a documented range, optionally check gcd(a, n) for a non-trivial factor, and use three-argument pow for the modular calculation. Stop immediately when a witness is found. Return or label the successful outcome as “probable prime,” not “proven prime,” and expose the number of rounds and base-selection rule in the function's contract. Add tests for negative values, 0, 1, 2, 3, even composites, odd composites, known primes, and Carmichael examples. Do not silently treat an exception or invalid range as a primality result.

Study instruction:
- Build a validation checklist and a unit-test set covering boundary values, witness discovery, repeated passing bases, and the distinction between probable-prime and proven-prime outcomes.

Key point:
- Validate the domain and base range before starting random trials.
- Make the probabilistic nature of a successful result explicit in the function interface.
38. Q: What is the time complexity of a fixed-round Fermat test, and why is it not literally constant-time for every input?
A: Show answer
If the algorithm performs a fixed number k of trials, it performs at most k modular exponentiation checks before returning a result. This makes the number of rounds independent of the numeric value of n. However, each modular exponentiation still depends on the exponent and the bit length of the operands. Efficient modular exponentiation uses repeated squaring and requires a number of modular multiplications that grows with the exponent's bit length; each multiplication also becomes more expensive as integers become larger. Therefore, the fixed trial count is not a claim that wall-clock time is constant for arbitrary-size integers. A more meaningful analysis separates the number of rounds from the bit complexity of each modular exponentiation. Memory use is generally modest compared with constructing the full power, but it also depends on operand size and implementation details.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a test with a fixed number of rounds as two layers: the number of trials and the cost of one modular exponentiation as the bit length of n grows.

Key point:
- A fixed trial count bounds the number of checks, not the total bit-level running time.
- Modular arithmetic remains more expensive as the candidate integer grows.
39. Q: Is the chapter's Fermat test sufficient for production cryptographic key generation?
A: Show answer
The chapter's simple Fermat test is useful for learning probabilistic primality testing, but it should not be treated as a complete production cryptographic control. Carmichael numbers demonstrate that a composite integer can satisfy the Fermat congruence for every coprime base, so simply increasing the number of Fermat rounds does not remove that structural weakness. Cryptographic systems require vetted primality-testing procedures, suitable random-number generation, well-defined parameter ranges, implementation review, and compliance with the applicable cryptographic standard or library requirements. Stronger probable-prime tests such as Miller–Rabin and Solovay–Strassen are identified in the chapter as alternatives that address the Carmichael problem, but production use should rely on a reviewed implementation rather than an improvised classroom function. A probable-prime result must also be distinguished from a formal primality proof where the application requires one.

Study instruction:
- Review the security requirements of a cryptographic use case and document why a textbook Fermat implementation is not an adequate standalone assurance mechanism.

Key point:
- Fermat testing is educational and useful for screening, but it has a known structural weakness.
- Use vetted cryptographic implementations and the assurance level required by the governing standard.
40. Q: How should an engineer decide between exact computation, random sampling, and a probabilistic primality test?
A: Show answer
Begin with the decision requirement: identify whether the output must be exact, whether an estimate is sufficient, and what types of error are tolerable. Next determine the size and structure of the input, the cost of an exact method, and whether a randomized method has a defensible error bound under the actual sampling design. For numerical analysis, choose a representative sample and report an uncertainty measure; for primality screening, use a test whose mathematical properties match the required assurance. Then define input validation, trial limits, stopping conditions, reproducibility needs, monitoring, and a fallback when confidence is inadequate. Record the algorithm version, parameters, random seed where appropriate, candidate or dataset scope, and result classification. A fast result without a clear statement of assumptions and limitations is not a reliable basis for a consequential decision.

Study instruction:
- Write a decision memo for a large-data estimate and a large-integer primality check, explaining why the selected method is appropriate and what additional verification is required.

Key point:
- Match the algorithm to the required assurance, not just the available runtime.
- Document assumptions, error limits, implementation controls, and fallback actions.
