Table 11
Substring Search, Sliding Windows, and Rabin–Karp Hashing
1. Q: What is substring search, and what are the pattern and text?
A: Show answer
Substring search asks whether a shorter sequence of characters occurs contiguously inside a longer sequence. The shorter sequence is the pattern, called the needle in the chapter; the larger sequence is the text, called the haystack. A search routine may return the first starting index, every matching index, or only a Boolean result. These are different output contracts and should be specified before choosing an algorithm. The chapter focuses on locating the first exact occurrence.

Study instruction:
- Write down the haystack, needle, expected result, and whether the task requires the first match or all matches. Trace the first occurrence by index.

Key point:
- Substring search is exact matching of a contiguous pattern within a text.

Source links:
- Python documentation: str.find
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
2. Q: How does a substring differ from a subsequence?
A: Show answer
A substring is contiguous: every character between its start and end positions is included. A subsequence preserves relative order but may skip characters. In the string “spider”, “ide” is a substring, while “sdr” is a subsequence but not a substring. Rabin–Karp and the chapter’s sliding-window examples compare contiguous windows, so allowing gaps would change the problem and invalidate the window-hash logic.

Study instruction:
- Classify several candidate strings against “spider”; explain which positions are skipped and why a skipped character disqualifies a substring.

Key point:
- Substring search never skips positions inside a candidate match.

Source links:
- Python documentation: str.find
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
3. Q: Which variables describe the size of a substring-search problem?
A: Show answer
Let H be the haystack length and M be the needle length. There are max(0, H − M + 1) possible starting positions for a fixed-length needle when M is positive. Using separate symbols matters because scanning the haystack and comparing a candidate window have different costs. Some literature uses n for text length and m for pattern length; the chapter uses H and N for haystack and needle in its brute-force discussion, then N and K in its window-sum example. State the convention explicitly in complexity analysis.

Study instruction:
- For H = 20 and M = 5, calculate the number of candidate windows and identify the final legal starting index.

Key point:
- Separate text length from pattern length when expressing time bounds.

Source links:
- Python documentation: str.find
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
4. Q: What should a first-match search function return?
A: Show answer
A clear contract is to return the lowest index at which the needle begins, or None when no occurrence exists. Python’s built-in str.find uses a different but equally explicit convention: it returns the lowest index or -1. The choice is an API decision, not an algorithmic difference. Callers must not confuse index zero with failure, because a match can legitimately begin at the first character.

Study instruction:
- Write tests for a match at index 0, a later match, and no match; assert the exact return value for the chosen API.

Key point:
- Document the failure sentinel and do not use a truthiness check for an index.

Source links:
- Python documentation: str.find
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
5. Q: How does brute-force substring search work?
A: Show answer
The algorithm tries every legal starting index in the haystack. At each start, it compares the needle and the corresponding haystack characters from left to right. A mismatch ends that candidate immediately; a complete sequence of equal characters proves a match. If every candidate fails, the function reports absence. The nested loops are simple, deterministic, and useful as a correctness baseline for more sophisticated algorithms.

Study instruction:
- Trace needle “bet” through “flibbertigibbet”; record each candidate start, the first mismatch, and the successful start.

Key point:
- Brute force checks candidate windows directly and stops each comparison at its first mismatch.

Source links:
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
- Knuth, Morris, and Pratt: Fast Pattern Matching in Strings
- Rivest: On the Worst-Case Behavior of String-Searching Algorithms
6. Q: Why does the brute-force loop stop at H − M + 1?
A: Show answer
A needle of length M can begin only where all M characters fit inside the haystack. The final valid start is H − M, inclusive. Python range excludes its endpoint, so range(H − M + 1) visits exactly the legal starts from zero through H − M. Omitting the +1 skips the final possible window; using H as the endpoint can cause out-of-range indexing.

Study instruction:
- For a haystack of length 8 and a needle of length 3, list the legal starts: 0 through 5. Confirm that the loop visits six positions.

Key point:
- Inclusive mathematical endpoints often require an exclusive Python range endpoint one larger.

Source links:
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
- Knuth, Morris, and Pratt: Fast Pattern Matching in Strings
- Rivest: On the Worst-Case Behavior of String-Searching Algorithms
7. Q: What is the worst-case time complexity of brute-force substring search?
A: Show answer
There are at most H − M + 1 candidate starts, and each candidate can compare as many as M characters. The worst-case bound is therefore O((H − M + 1)M), commonly simplified to O(HM). Inputs with long repeated prefixes make many candidates almost match before failing. For example, a needle like “aaaab” against a long run of “a” characters repeatedly performs nearly M comparisons.

Study instruction:
- Construct a family of inputs with a long run of repeated characters and count comparisons as M and H grow.

Key point:
- A simple nested comparison can be quadratic in the two input lengths.

Source links:
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
- Knuth, Morris, and Pratt: Fast Pattern Matching in Strings
- Rivest: On the Worst-Case Behavior of String-Searching Algorithms
8. Q: Why can brute-force search still be fast on ordinary data?
A: Show answer
Many candidate starts fail on the first or second character, so the inner loop rarely scans the full needle. Its practical running time can be close to linear for favorable text distributions. This average behavior does not remove the O(HM) worst case, and it should not be used as a guarantee for adversarial or highly repetitive data. Measure representative inputs rather than inferring performance from one demonstration.

Study instruction:
- Benchmark random text and repetitive text with the same H and M; compare character-comparison counts rather than only wall-clock time.

Key point:
- Early mismatches help average behavior, but worst-case analysis still matters.

Source links:
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
- Knuth, Morris, and Pratt: Fast Pattern Matching in Strings
- Rivest: On the Worst-Case Behavior of String-Searching Algorithms
9. Q: How do KMP, Boyer–Moore, and Rabin–Karp differ at a high level?
A: Show answer
KMP preprocesses the pattern to reuse information from partial matches; Boyer–Moore uses mismatch information to skip candidate positions, often performing well on ordinary text; Rabin–Karp compares rolling fingerprints of fixed-length windows. Their preprocessing, memory use, alphabet assumptions, and worst-case guarantees differ by variant. The chapter introduces these names as alternatives, but does not implement KMP or Boyer–Moore. Do not assume every algorithm bearing the Boyer–Moore name has identical worst-case bounds.

Study instruction:
- Summarize what each algorithm reuses after a mismatch: prefix information, shift information, or a rolling fingerprint.

Key point:
- Choose by workload and guarantee, not by the algorithm’s name alone.

Source links:
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
- Knuth, Morris, and Pratt: Fast Pattern Matching in Strings
- Rivest: On the Worst-Case Behavior of String-Searching Algorithms
10. Q: When is brute-force search or Python’s built-in find appropriate?
A: Show answer
Brute force is appropriate for small inputs, one-off searches, educational implementations, and test oracles. Its low implementation complexity reduces opportunities for bugs. For ordinary Python exact substring lookup, str.find is usually the first choice because it is implemented and optimized by the runtime. A custom algorithm is justified when it provides a needed capability, such as streaming, custom tokenization, multiple patterns, or instrumentation.

Study instruction:
- Compare a short custom implementation with str.find on realistic data; identify whether the custom version provides a concrete requirement.

Key point:
- Prefer the simplest correct implementation that meets the workload.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
11. Q: What is a fixed-size sliding window?
A: Show answer
A fixed-size sliding window is a contiguous range of K elements that moves through a sequence one position at a time while keeping its length unchanged. Adjacent windows overlap in K − 1 elements: one element leaves on the left and one enters on the right. This overlap is the source of the efficiency gain because most of the previous window’s work remains useful.

Study instruction:
- Draw the windows of length four over [3, 2, 7, 4, 6, 3, 5, 8] and mark the outgoing and incoming values at each move.

Key point:
- A fixed window preserves its length and updates only at its boundaries.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
12. Q: Why is recomputing every window sum inefficient?
A: Show answer
There are approximately N candidate windows, and summing each one from scratch requires K additions. The total work is O(NK). The overlap between consecutive windows is ignored, so most values are added repeatedly. The cost grows with both the sequence length and the window length, even though only two values differ between neighboring windows.

Study instruction:
- Count how many additions are needed to sum every length-K window naively, then compare that count with a rolling update.

Key point:
- Repeatedly recomputing shared work creates avoidable cost.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
13. Q: How does the sliding-window sum update work?
A: Show answer
First compute the sum of the initial K elements. For each next position, add the incoming element and subtract the element that just left the window: new_sum = old_sum + incoming − outgoing. Update the maximum after each new sum. The update performs a constant number of arithmetic operations regardless of K, provided the arithmetic operations themselves are treated as constant time.

Study instruction:
- Manually calculate the successive sums for the chapter’s array and verify the maximum is 22 for the length-four windows.

Key point:
- Reuse the previous aggregate by removing what leaves and adding what enters.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
14. Q: Why does the fixed-size sliding-window algorithm run in O(N) time?
A: Show answer
The initial window requires K additions. The remaining N − K elements are processed once each, with one addition and one subtraction per step. The total is O(K + N − K), which simplifies to O(N). The algorithm uses a constant number of state variables, so its auxiliary space is O(1). This is a standard linear scan with constant work per incoming element.

Study instruction:
- Write the operation count as K + 2(N − K) and simplify it; distinguish input storage from auxiliary storage.

Key point:
- Linear time follows because each new element triggers constant update work.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
15. Q: What preconditions must a fixed-window sum function check?
A: Show answer
The input must contain at least K elements, and K must be positive if the problem defines a nonempty window. The chapter’s four-element function indexes the first four values immediately, so a shorter list raises IndexError. A reusable function should validate K, define behavior for an empty sequence, and decide whether K greater than the sequence length is an error or a no-window result.

Study instruction:
- Test an empty list, a list shorter than K, a list exactly K long, and K equal to zero; document each expected behavior.

Key point:
- Validate window size before indexing the initial window.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
16. Q: How should the maximum-window-sum algorithm handle negative values?
A: Show answer
Initialize the maximum to the sum of the first valid window, not to zero. If every value is negative, zero is not the sum of any valid window and would produce an incorrect answer. The same initialization pattern applies to maximum averages or other fixed-window aggregates when the domain can contain negative values.

Study instruction:
- Use an array containing only negative numbers and confirm that the returned maximum is also negative.

Key point:
- Initialize an optimum from a real candidate when all candidates may be negative.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
17. Q: What is the difference between fixed and variable sliding windows?
A: Show answer
A fixed window always has the same number of elements and moves its left and right boundaries together. A variable window expands as new elements are included and contracts when a validity condition is broken. Fixed windows are natural for exactly K elements or characters; variable windows are useful for constraints such as no repeated characters or at most K distinct values. Both rely on contiguous ranges and incremental state updates.

Study instruction:
- Classify the maximum three-vowel problem as fixed-size and the longest no-duplicate substring problem as variable-size.

Key point:
- The constraint determines whether the window length stays fixed or changes.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
18. Q: How does the fixed three-character vowel-count exercise work?
A: Show answer
Count vowels in the first three characters, then slide one character at a time. For each move, subtract one if the outgoing character is a vowel and add one if the incoming character is a vowel. Track the largest count. The algorithm runs in O(L) time for a string of length L and uses O(1) auxiliary space. Define the vowel set explicitly as a, e, i, o, and u if following the exercise’s convention.

Study instruction:
- Trace “spider” and “beautiful”; verify maxima of 2 and 3 respectively. Include a string shorter than three characters in your tests.

Key point:
- A fixed character window can maintain a count with one removal and one addition.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
19. Q: How can the longest substring without duplicate characters be solved with a variable window?
A: Show answer
Maintain a left boundary and scan the string with a right boundary. Keep a mapping from each character to its most recent index. When the current character was last seen inside the active window, move left to one position after that earlier occurrence. Then update the last-seen index and compute right − left + 1. The maximum of these valid window lengths is the answer.

Study instruction:
- Trace “ababcdabca” and record left, right, last-seen positions, and the best length after each character.

Key point:
- Expand right, and move left only far enough to restore uniqueness.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
20. Q: Why must the left boundary never move backwards in the variable-window algorithm?
A: Show answer
A previous occurrence may lie before the current window. Moving left to that old index plus one would reintroduce characters that have already been excluded and can make the window invalid. Use left = max(left, previous_index + 1), or explicitly check that previous_index is at least left. This monotonicity is central to the linear-time proof.

Study instruction:
- Use “abba” to demonstrate the bug caused by moving left backwards; then repair it with max.

Key point:
- A stale last-seen index must not move the active window to the left.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
21. Q: What is the time and space complexity of the longest unique-substring algorithm?
A: Show answer
Each character is processed once by the right pointer. The left pointer only advances, so its total movement is at most the string length. Dictionary lookups and updates are expected O(1), giving expected O(L) time. The last-seen dictionary can contain up to the number of distinct characters, so auxiliary space is O(min(L, alphabet size)) in a bounded alphabet and O(L) in the general case.

Study instruction:
- Explain why the pointer movements do not create O(L²) work; count total pointer advances.

Key point:
- Monotonic boundaries make the variable-window scan linear in total pointer movement.

Source links:
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
- Python documentation: text sequence type str
22. Q: How should a variable window compute its current length?
A: Show answer
For inclusive boundaries left and right, the length is right − left + 1. If a half-open convention is used, with right positioned one past the last character, the length is right − left. Mixing these conventions creates off-by-one errors. The chapter’s example starts at index 2 and identifies “abcd” as length four, illustrating the inclusive calculation.

Study instruction:
- Write both boundary conventions and test a one-character window; its length must be one.

Key point:
- Choose inclusive or half-open boundaries and apply the matching length formula consistently.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
23. Q: What does Rabin–Karp do differently from direct character comparison?
A: Show answer
Rabin–Karp maps the pattern and each same-length text window to compact integer fingerprints. Unequal fingerprints prove that the strings differ; equal fingerprints indicate a candidate match. A rolling-hash formula updates the next window fingerprint from the previous one without reprocessing all M characters. If fingerprints can collide, direct verification is needed before reporting an exact match.

Study instruction:
- Draw the pattern hash and the first two window hashes; identify when a character comparison is actually required.

Key point:
- Rabin–Karp replaces most full string comparisons with fingerprint comparisons.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
24. Q: Why must the Rabin–Karp window have the same length as the pattern?
A: Show answer
The hash values are intended to represent candidate strings of the same number of symbols. If the window length differs, the positional weights differ and equal numeric values no longer encode the same fixed-length sequence under the same interpretation. The search therefore initializes a window of length M and slides it by one character until the final legal start H − M.

Study instruction:
- For a pattern of length four, list the first, second, and final four-character windows of a sample haystack.

Key point:
- Compare only equal-length candidate windows.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
25. Q: How is a string represented as a positional-number value?
A: Show answer
Assign each alphabet symbol a digit value and interpret the sequence as a number in a chosen base. For digits x0 through x(M−1) and base b, the value is x0·b^(M−1) + x1·b^(M−2) + … + x(M−1). This polynomial representation preserves order and position; simply adding character codes would lose positional information.

Study instruction:
- Calculate the base-26 value of “cab” using a=0, b=1, c=2; confirm that it is 2·26² + 0·26 + 1.

Key point:
- A positional polynomial hash encodes both the symbols and their order.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
26. Q: Why is the base-10 digit scheme unsafe for an alphabet larger than ten symbols?
A: Show answer
If a symbol is mapped to a value greater than nine, its representation occupies more than one decimal digit. Different sequences can then produce the same concatenated decimal numeral even before any modular reduction. For example, the symbol k mapped to 10 can be confused with the two symbols b and a mapped to 1 and 0. This is an encoding ambiguity, not a collision caused by a prime modulus.

Study instruction:
- Compare the naive decimal encodings of the one-symbol string “k” and the two-symbol string “ba”; explain why the representation is ambiguous.

Key point:
- Each alphabet symbol must occupy exactly one digit position in the chosen base.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
27. Q: Why does base 26 solve the lowercase-English-letter digit-bleeding problem?
A: Show answer
With a=0 through z=25, every symbol is a valid single digit in base 26. Each character therefore occupies exactly one positional place, so a fixed-length string has an unambiguous positional value before modular compression. More generally, choose a base at least as large as the alphabet size when digits are numbered from zero.

Study instruction:
- Convert “bmm” to decimal using 1·26² + 12·26 + 12; verify the result is 1000.

Key point:
- Choose a base that can represent every symbol as one digit.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
28. Q: How does the rolling hash remove the outgoing character and append the incoming one?
A: Show answer
For a window of length M, let h be its polynomial value in base b, and let x be the outgoing digit. Remove its highest-place contribution x·b^(M−1), multiply the remainder by b to shift all remaining digits left, then add the incoming digit y. Thus h_next = (h − x·b^(M−1))·b + y. This takes a constant number of arithmetic operations when the values are bounded.

Study instruction:
- Use the base-10 example 3420, remove the leading 3, multiply the remaining 420 by 10, and append 5 to obtain 4205.

Key point:
- A rolling hash updates the fingerprint by removing the old leading contribution and appending the new digit.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
29. Q: What is the drop-place value in a rolling hash?
A: Show answer
The drop-place value is b^(M−1), the positional weight of the outgoing leftmost symbol in a window of length M. Its product with the outgoing digit is the contribution removed before shifting the remaining value. When modular hashing is used, precompute d = b^(M−1) mod q so the outgoing contribution can be removed modulo q without constructing a huge power.

Study instruction:
- For base 26 and a four-character window, identify the drop-place exponent as three; compute the modular value by repeated multiplication.

Key point:
- Precompute the highest positional weight once for all window updates.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- MIT OpenCourseWare: Karp–Rabin and rolling hashes
30. Q: Why is the initial hash computed separately from the rolling hash?
A: Show answer
The first window has no preceding window from which to derive its value, so its fingerprint must be computed by scanning its M characters. The pattern fingerprint is computed in the same way. Once the first text window is known, every subsequent fingerprint can be derived from the previous one. The total initialization cost is O(M), followed by constant-time hash updates per position in the modular arithmetic model.

Study instruction:
- Identify the two initial hashes and the first rolling update in a trace of Rabin–Karp.

Key point:
- Hash the pattern and first window from scratch; roll only after that.

- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- Karp and Rabin: Efficient randomized pattern-matching algorithms
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
31. Q: How does division hashing keep Rabin–Karp fingerprints small?
A: Show answer
Division hashing reduces a large polynomial value to a residue modulo a chosen positive modulus q, commonly a prime. The fingerprint is h mod q and therefore lies between zero and q−1 for positive q. This bounds the size of stored hash values and keeps arithmetic manageable. The modulus does not preserve unique identity: distinct strings can map to the same residue.

Study instruction:
- For q=613, compute 35286 mod 613 and verify that the residue is 345.

Key point:
- Modulo reduction compresses a large polynomial value into a bounded fingerprint.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- Karp and Rabin: Efficient randomized pattern-matching algorithms
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
32. Q: Why can division hashing create collisions?
A: Show answer
There are infinitely many possible integer values but only q possible residues modulo q. By the pigeonhole principle, distinct values must share residues when enough distinct inputs are considered. If two different fixed-length windows have the same fingerprint, the algorithm may treat them as a candidate match even though their characters differ. This is a false positive unless the candidate is verified.

Study instruction:
- Use two distinct integer values with the same remainder modulo a small q; explain how they produce the same fingerprint.

Key point:
- A short fingerprint cannot uniquely identify every possible long string.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- Karp and Rabin: Efficient randomized pattern-matching algorithms
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
33. Q: Can a hash collision cause a false negative in exact Rabin–Karp?
A: Show answer
No, not when the same deterministic hash function is applied consistently to the pattern and every window. Identical strings necessarily produce identical fingerprints, so a true occurrence will be a hash match. A collision can create a false positive by making different strings look equal, but it cannot make identical strings hash differently under the same computation.

Study instruction:
- Prove the one-way implication: equal strings imply equal hashes; then explain why the converse fails after modular compression.

Key point:
- Hash equality is necessary for a match but is not sufficient for exact equality.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- Karp and Rabin: Efficient randomized pattern-matching algorithms
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
34. Q: Is the probability of a collision always exactly 1 divided by the modulus?
A: Show answer
No. That is a simplifying intuition, not a universal guarantee for a fixed hash function and arbitrary inputs. A rigorous probability bound depends on the hash family, how its parameters are randomized, the input lengths, and whether inputs may be adversarial. Standard Karp–Rabin analyses choose a random prime from a specified range and use number-theoretic bounds plus a union bound across all candidate windows. A fixed small prime such as 613 should not be treated as a production-grade probability guarantee.

Study instruction:
- Compare the chapter’s illustrative 1/q intuition with the randomized-prime analysis in the university notes; identify the assumptions needed for a bound.

Key point:
- Collision probability requires a stated randomization model and input assumptions.

- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- Karp and Rabin: Efficient randomized pattern-matching algorithms
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
35. Q: Why does the total search error probability depend on the number of windows?
A: Show answer
Even if each comparison has a small false-positive probability, the algorithm performs many comparisons. The probability that at least one false positive occurs across the search can therefore grow with the number of windows. A union bound adds the individual upper bounds, giving a conservative total-error bound. The modulus or hash parameters should be chosen against the entire search workload, not a single comparison in isolation.

Study instruction:
- If each of W windows has a proven error bound ε, state the union-bound total as at most Wε, capped at one.

Key point:
- More candidate windows create more opportunities for a false positive.

36. Q: What makes a randomized Rabin–Karp variant a Monte Carlo algorithm?
A: Show answer
A Monte Carlo algorithm has a bounded or controlled running time but may return an incorrect result with a small probability. In a fingerprint-only version, a collision can cause a nonmatching window to be reported as a match. The probability can be reduced by using an appropriately randomized hash family, larger parameter ranges, or independent fingerprints. The guarantee must be stated quantitatively rather than described merely as unlikely.

Study instruction:
- Describe the algorithm’s runtime guarantee and its possible error separately; identify which output can be wrong.

Key point:
- Monte Carlo trades a quantified error probability for efficiency.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- Karp and Rabin: Efficient randomized pattern-matching algorithms
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
37. Q: How does candidate verification turn Rabin–Karp into a Las Vegas-style exact search?
A: Show answer
Whenever the pattern and window fingerprints match, compare the actual characters. Return a match only if this verification succeeds; otherwise continue scanning. A collision can then cause extra work but cannot cause an incorrect reported occurrence. The algorithm is always correct, while its runtime depends on how many false fingerprint matches require verification. This is the Las Vegas form described in the chapter.

Study instruction:
- Create a deliberate collision with a small modulus and show that direct comparison rejects it.

Key point:
- Verification converts a hash match from a conclusion into a candidate requiring proof.

- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes

- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
38. Q: What is the worst-case complexity after adding verification?
A: Show answer
Hash initialization and rolling updates take O(H+M) under the bounded-arithmetic model. However, if many windows collide and each verification scans M characters, verification can add O(HM) work in the worst case. With a suitable randomized hash family, the expected number of false candidates can be kept small, yielding expected near-linear performance. State the deterministic worst-case and expected randomized bounds separately.

Study instruction:
- Construct a tiny-modulus example that makes many windows share a fingerprint; count verification work.

Key point:
- Exact verification guarantees correctness, but not an unconditional linear worst-case bound.

39. Q: How does modular arithmetic let the initial hash stay small?
A: Show answer
Instead of computing the full polynomial and taking one remainder at the end, reduce after every character. Start with the first digit modulo q; for each next digit x, set h = (h·b + x) mod q. The identity that congruent values remain congruent under addition and multiplication guarantees the same final residue. Every intermediate hash stays in the range zero through q−1 when q is positive.

Study instruction:
- Compute the hash of “cafe” with base 26 and q=613 one character at a time; compare the result with the full-value remainder.

Key point:
- Reduce at each step to avoid constructing an enormous integer.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- Karp and Rabin: Efficient randomized pattern-matching algorithms
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
40. Q: What is the modular rolling-hash formula?
A: Show answer
Let h be the previous window hash, x the outgoing digit, y the incoming digit, b the base, q the modulus, and d = b^(M−1) mod q. Then h_next = ((h − x·d)·b + y) mod q. In Python, use the percent operator after the subtraction and multiplication; Python’s modulo result for a positive divisor is nonnegative even when the intermediate value is negative. The formula is the modular version of the remove-shift-append derivation.

Study instruction:
- Implement the formula with a small base and modulus, then compare every rolled hash with a fresh hash of the corresponding slice.

Key point:
- The rolling update and initial hash must use identical digit mapping, base, and modulus.

41. Q: How can the drop-place remainder be computed without a huge power?
A: Show answer
Compute d = b^(M−1) mod q using repeated modular multiplication, or use Python’s three-argument pow(b, M−1, q). The latter computes modular exponentiation without first constructing b^(M−1) as a huge integer. Precompute d once per pattern length and reuse it for every window update.

Study instruction:
- Compare pow(26, 499, 613) with a loop that multiplies by 26 and reduces modulo 613 at every step.

Key point:
- Modular exponentiation keeps the intermediate values bounded.

42. Q: What assumptions are hidden in the chapter’s character_hash_code function?
A: Show answer
The expression ord(char) − 97 maps lowercase ASCII letters a through z to values zero through 25. It does not correctly map uppercase letters, digits, punctuation, spaces, or arbitrary Unicode characters into a nonnegative base-26 digit range. Increasing the base does not by itself fix the mapping; the character-code function must also change.

43. Q: How can Rabin–Karp support a larger or Unicode alphabet?
A: Show answer
Define a stable mapping from each symbol to an integer digit in the range zero through b−1, where b is at least the alphabet’s cardinality. For a bounded byte alphabet, use byte values and base 256. For Python str, ord returns a Unicode code point, so a direct code-point polynomial can use a base larger than the maximum allowed code point, or a deliberate encoding or token mapping can be used. Ensure that the same representation is used for both strings.

Study instruction:
- Design and test a mapping for mixed case, digits, punctuation, and non-ASCII text; document whether matching is by code point or encoded byte.

- Unicode requires an explicit symbol model; lowercase-English assumptions are not general text support.

44. Q: What is the difference between searching Python str and searching encoded bytes?
A: Show answer
Python str is a sequence of Unicode code points; bytes is a sequence of byte values from zero through 255. A match in str is defined over code points, while a match in bytes is defined over bytes. UTF-8 encodes one code point using one to four bytes, so byte offsets and character indices can differ. If the application reports user-visible character positions, search the decoded str or maintain a byte-to-character index mapping.

Study instruction:
- Search for a non-ASCII character in both a str and its UTF-8 encoding; compare the reported offsets.

Key point:
- Choose code-point or byte semantics before designing the index contract.

45. Q: What constraints apply to the base and modulus?
A: Show answer
The base must be at least the number of distinct mapped digits when using a positional encoding, and every digit must lie between zero and base−1. The modulus q must be positive and nonzero; prime moduli are common in randomized polynomial hashing because they support useful mathematical bounds. A poor or tiny modulus increases collisions. The base and modulus are algorithm parameters and should be selected and tested together.

Study instruction:
- Add assertions for base greater than the maximum digit and modulus greater than one; test invalid values.

Key point:
- Valid digit ranges and a positive modulus are essential invariants.

46. Q: How should an empty needle be handled?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s sample code indexes the first character of the needle during initial hashing, so an empty needle raises an error. A production API should explicitly define the conventional behavior. Python’s str.find treats the empty string as found at index zero. A custom function may adopt that convention or reject empty patterns, but it must do so before indexing or hashing.

47. Q: What should happen when the needle is longer than the haystack?
A: Show answer
No fixed-length window can contain the needle, so the search should return the not-found result immediately. This check also prevents slicing or indexing assumptions from becoming part of the algorithm’s accidental behavior. It can be performed before any hash computation, in O(1) time after lengths are known.

Study instruction:
- Test a five-character needle against a three-character haystack and confirm no initial window is accessed.

Key point:
- If M exceeds H, there are zero candidate windows.

48. Q: Why should a Rabin–Karp implementation check the first window separately?
A: Show answer
A common implementation hashes the first window, compares it with the pattern hash, and returns zero if they match after verification. The rolling loop then begins at start index one. Forgetting the initial comparison misses a match at index zero. Conversely, starting the rolling loop at zero can accidentally drop a character that has not yet been represented by a previous window.

Study instruction:
- Test a pattern that appears only at index zero and another that appears only at the final legal index.

Key point:
- The first window is initialized, not generated by a rolling update.

49. Q: How can a robust exact Rabin–Karp routine be structured in Python?
A: Show answer
Validate the input contract, handle an empty pattern and a pattern longer than the text, compute the pattern and first-window hashes, and precompute the drop-place remainder. Compare the first window, then roll through each remaining legal start. Whenever hashes match, verify the candidate slice before returning. Keep the character mapping, base, and modulus in one controlled configuration rather than scattered globals. This separates correctness conditions from the optimization.

50. Q: Why can slicing during candidate verification affect memory use?
A: Show answer
A comparison such as needle == haystack[i:i+M] creates a new string slice, so a verification may allocate temporary memory proportional to M. The rolling-hash state itself uses O(1) auxiliary space, but the complete Python implementation may temporarily allocate an O(M) slice. A character-by-character verifier can avoid the slice allocation, though it still performs up to M comparisons. State the implementation-level space claim precisely.

Study instruction:
- Compare a slice-based verifier with an indexed character loop; inspect allocation behavior for a long candidate.

Key point:
- Constant rolling-hash state does not mean every Python operation is allocation-free.

51. Q: What does O(H+M) mean for the modular Rabin–Karp search?
A: Show answer
The pattern and first text window each require O(M) hashing. There are at most H−M subsequent windows, each updated with a constant number of modular operations. Thus the hash-scanning phase takes O(H+M). This bound assumes the modulus is small enough that each modular arithmetic operation is treated as constant time, as in the word-RAM analysis. Python big-integer costs depend on operand bit length, so a modulus that grows with the input requires a more careful bit-complexity model.

Study instruction:
- Separate initialization cost from per-window cost and state the arithmetic model used for the bound.

Key point:
- Linear scanning relies on bounded-size fingerprints and constant-time modular operations.

52. Q: How much extra space does Rabin–Karp use?
A: Show answer
The rolling search stores a small number of integers: the pattern hash, current window hash, modulus, base, and drop-place remainder. This is O(1) auxiliary state beyond the input. A randomized implementation may also store random parameters. A slice-based verification temporarily allocates a candidate substring, while an indexed verifier does not. The chapter contrasts Rabin–Karp’s small auxiliary state with preprocessing tables used by some other algorithms.

53. Q: What is a practical reason to use a large or randomized prime modulus?
A: Show answer
A larger, properly selected modulus provides a larger fingerprint space and can reduce collision probability under the randomized-hash analysis. Random selection from a mathematically justified range avoids relying on a single hand-picked constant that may interact badly with structured or adversarial input. The precise error bound depends on the random-prime selection scheme and number of candidate comparisons; merely choosing a visually large prime does not establish a formal guarantee.

54. Q: How can multiple independent fingerprints reduce false-positive risk?
A: Show answer
Compute independent fingerprints using independently selected parameters and accept a candidate only when all fingerprints match. If the events are independent and each has a proven false-positive probability ε, the joint probability is ε raised to the number of independent fingerprints. Independence and the per-hash bound are essential; simply using two correlated moduli does not automatically justify multiplying probabilities.

Study instruction:
- For two genuinely independent hash choices with a per-choice bound ε, derive the joint bound and list the independence assumption.

Key point:
- Independent checks can reduce error probability multiplicatively when the analysis supports it.

55. Q: How should a collision be logged or diagnosed?
A: Show answer
Record the pattern position, candidate position, fingerprint parameters, and the fact that the character verification failed. Do not log sensitive full text by default; a position and diagnostic metadata may be enough. A high collision-verification rate can indicate a weak modulus, poor parameter selection, repetitive input, or an implementation bug in the rolling update. Compare rolled hashes with freshly recomputed hashes to distinguish parameter quality from arithmetic errors.

Study instruction:
- Instrument a test version to count hash matches, verified matches, and rejected collisions; compare with fresh-hash recomputation.

Key point:

56. Q: How can the rolling-hash implementation be tested against a trusted oracle?
A: Show answer
For small randomized strings, compare the custom function’s result with Python’s str.find. Also recompute every window hash from scratch and compare it with the rolling value at that position. Include repeated characters, overlapping matches, no match, match at the first and last positions, empty input, and a tiny modulus that deliberately creates collisions. Differential testing is especially effective for catching off-by-one errors in outgoing and incoming indices.

57. Q: Which off-by-one errors are common in Rabin–Karp?
A: Show answer
Typical errors include omitting the final legal window, using the wrong outgoing index, reading the incoming character one position too early or too late, using M rather than M−1 as the drop exponent, and failing to compare the initial window. The outgoing character for a new window beginning at i is haystack[i−1]; the incoming character is haystack[i+M−1]. These indices should be derived from the window boundaries rather than memorized.

Study instruction:
- For a window beginning at i, write its old and new index ranges and identify the single outgoing and incoming positions.

Key point:
- Derive rolling indices from the window definition to avoid boundary mistakes.

58. Q: Why can a wrong rolling formula silently corrupt every later window?
A: Show answer
Each rolling hash depends on the previous hash. If one update uses the wrong outgoing digit or drop-place weight, all subsequent fingerprints may diverge from their true values. This can create missed matches or spurious candidate matches even though the modular arithmetic itself is valid. A useful invariant is that the stored hash must equal a fresh hash of the current window after every slide.

Study instruction:
- Add a debug assertion that compares each rolled hash with a fresh initial hash of the current window on short test strings.

Key point:
- Check the rolling invariant after every move during development.

- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
- Python documentation: str.find
59. Q: How does string division hashing relate to a hash table?
A: Show answer
Both convert values into bounded hash codes using modular arithmetic, and both must handle collisions. In a hash table, a collision is resolved by a collision-management strategy such as chaining or probing while preserving key equality. In exact substring search, a matching fingerprint is treated only as a candidate and the underlying window is verified. The same string-hash polynomial can therefore be reused in a string-keyed DivisionHasher, provided the key equality and collision strategy are correct.

Study instruction:
- Adapt the initial modular hash to string keys in a hash-table design; explain where key equality is checked.

Key point:
- Hashing narrows the search space; it does not replace equality checks.

- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
- Python documentation: str.find
60. Q: Why does a positional polynomial hash distinguish anagrams better than multiplying character codes?
A: Show answer
A polynomial hash assigns different positional weights to characters, so changing character order usually changes the result. Multiplying all character codes together is commutative, meaning anagrams produce the same product even when their order differs. A positional hash is therefore more suitable for sequence matching, although modular reduction can still create collisions.

Study instruction:
- Compare two anagrams under a product-based hash and a positional polynomial hash; explain which preserves order information.

Key point:
- Substring hashing must encode character position as well as character identity.

- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
- Python documentation: str.find
61. Q: How does Rabin–Karp compare with Python’s built-in str.find in everyday applications?
A: Show answer
str.find already returns the lowest matching index or −1 and is the normal choice for a simple exact search. A custom Rabin–Karp implementation is useful for learning rolling hashes, searching streams, extending the idea to multiple patterns or multidimensional data, or controlling fingerprint behavior. It is not automatically faster than Python’s optimized implementation; pure-Python loops may be slower due to interpreter overhead.

62. Q: What are the most important production safeguards for a custom Rabin–Karp search?
A: Show answer
Validate empty and oversized patterns, use a documented character encoding, select parameters with a justified collision analysis, verify every matching fingerprint, and test overlapping matches and boundary positions. Avoid fixed weak parameters for adversarial input, monitor collision-verification rates, and benchmark against str.find. If text is streamed, define how the rolling window crosses chunk boundaries and retain the last M−1 symbols between chunks.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a deployment checklist covering input limits, Unicode semantics, parameter selection, exact verification, observability, and chunk boundaries.

Key point:
- A production search routine needs explicit semantics, collision safety, and operational tests.

Source links:
- Carnegie Mellon: Fingerprinting and String Matching lecture notes
- University of Tennessee: Rabin–Karp string-searching notes
- Python documentation: str.find

Table 12
Sets, Boolean Arrays, Bit Vectors, and Bit Manipulation
1. Q: What is a set, and why is it useful in algorithms?
A: Show answer
A set is a collection of distinct values: each value is either present or absent, and duplicate occurrences do not create additional members. This makes sets useful for membership tests, duplicate detection, removing repeated values, and representing categories already encountered. The central design question is how to represent membership. A hash-based set is flexible for many hashable value types; a Boolean array or bit vector can be more suitable when values are integers from a known, reasonably compact range. Set membership and set storage should be chosen together with the expected workload and data distribution.

Study instruction: List three tasks in which membership matters more than the order of the input, and identify a suitable set representation for each.

Key point: A set models membership, not multiplicity or input order.

Source links: Python set and frozenset types
2. Q: Why does the nested-loop approach to duplicate detection take O(N²) time?
A: Show answer
For each element, the algorithm scans later elements to see whether the same value appears again. In an input with no duplicates, it performs approximately N(N−1)/2 comparisons. The number of comparisons therefore grows quadratically as the input grows. The approach can use O(1) auxiliary space, but that space advantage is purchased with substantially more work. It may still be reasonable for very small arrays or when an explicit no-extra-memory constraint outweighs speed.

Study instruction: Count the comparisons made by the nested-loop method for arrays of lengths 4, 8, and 16, assuming no duplicates.

Key point: Quadratic comparison work can be the price of constant auxiliary space.

Source links: NIST Dictionary of Algorithms and Data Structures
3. Q: How does a hash-based set make duplicate detection linear on average?
A: Show answer
Scan the input once while maintaining the values already encountered. For each item, test membership; if it is present, return True immediately. Otherwise insert it and continue. With expected constant-time hash-table membership and insertion, N items require expected O(N) total work and O(N) auxiliary storage. If the scan finishes, no duplicate was found. In Python, use a built-in set and write if item in seen rather than creating a dictionary solely to store True values. The expected-time qualification matters: hash-table operations are not an unconditional worst-case O(1) guarantee.

Study instruction: Trace the set after processing [4, 3, 5, 1, 3] and identify the exact iteration on which the function returns.

Key point: One pass plus expected constant-time membership gives expected O(N) detection.

Source links: Python set operations; Python dictionaries
4. Q: How should the data structure for a set be selected?
A: Show answer
Consider the value domain, number of distinct members, density, required operations, memory limit, and whether values can be mapped directly to indexes. A hash set is usually the flexible default for strings, objects, and widely scattered integers. A Boolean array is attractive for a compact integer range when direct indexing is valuable. A bit vector is attractive when that range is large enough for Boolean slots to be wasteful but still compact enough to represent every possible value. Measure the actual workload rather than selecting solely from asymptotic notation.

Study instruction: Compare a hash set, Boolean array, and bit vector for five values drawn from a range of one million and for 800 values drawn from a range of 1,000.

Key point: Domain size and set density are as important as element count.

Source links: Python sets; Python array module
5. Q: What Python set constraints arise from hashing and equality?
A: Show answer
Python set members must be hashable. Their hash value must remain consistent while they are members, and objects that compare equal must have equal hash values. Immutable values such as integers, strings, and suitable tuples are common members; a list or dictionary cannot be inserted directly because it is mutable and unhashable. Custom classes must implement hashing and equality consistently. These rules matter when duplicate detection is generalized from integers to arbitrary objects: the algorithm relies on the object's equality and hash behavior to define what counts as a duplicate.

Study instruction: Try inserting an integer, a tuple, and a list into a Python set; explain why the list fails and what immutable representation could replace it.

Key point: Hash-based membership depends on stable, consistent hashing and equality.

Source links: Python set types; Python data model: hashing
6. Q: Under what conditions is counting sort appropriate?
A: Show answer
Counting sort is useful when keys are integers, or can be mapped to integer indexes, and the key range R is manageable relative to the number of input items N. Unlike comparison sorting, it exploits the known domain rather than comparing every pair or ordering keys through comparisons. Its efficiency depends on both N and R. If the range is enormous and the input is small, initializing or scanning the domain can cost more than comparison sorting. The chapter's presence-set variant also assumes a defined range and must be adapted when negative values or duplicates are present.

Study instruction: For each proposed workload, estimate N and R before deciding whether counting sort is plausible.

Key point: Counting sort trades a bounded key domain for reduced comparison work.

Source links: NIST: Counting sort
7. Q: How does the chapter's set-based counting-sort variant work?
A: Show answer
First create an empty membership structure. Scan the input and mark each value as present. Then scan the entire permitted range in ascending order; whenever a value is marked, append it to the result. Because the second scan visits values in sorted order, the result is sorted without comparing input values with one another. When the structure records only presence, the result contains each distinct value once. It therefore sorts a set of keys rather than preserving repeated occurrences unless the representation is changed to store frequencies.

Study instruction: Trace the algorithm on [860, 2345, 9999] using a small illustrative range and write the values emitted by the second scan.

Key point: Mark membership, then enumerate the domain in ascending order.

Source links: NIST: Counting sort
8. Q: Why is the time complexity of counting sort expressed as O(N + R)?
A: Show answer
The input scan processes N values and records their presence or frequency. The range scan examines R possible keys to emit the result in order. The combined work is proportional to N plus R, assuming constant-time updates or lookups in the chosen direct-address structure. This can outperform O(N log N) comparison sorting when R is not excessively larger than N. It is not accurate to describe counting sort as simply O(N) without stating the range assumption, because the domain scan is part of the algorithm.

Study instruction: Compare N + R with N log₂N for N = 5,000 and R = 10,000, then repeat with R = 1,000,000.

Key point: The range scan is a real cost and must be included.

Source links: NIST: Counting sort
9. Q: How can counting sort preserve duplicate values?
A: Show answer
Store a frequency for each key instead of a Boolean presence flag. Each input occurrence increments the corresponding count. During the ascending range scan, append each key as many times as its count indicates. For example, [7, 1, 1, 1, 4, 4] produces counts of three for 1, two for 4, and one for 7, so the output is [1, 1, 1, 4, 4, 7]. This produces sorted keys with multiplicity preserved. A full stable counting sort for records requires additional cumulative-count and placement steps; a frequency-only version does not preserve the original order of associated records.

Study instruction: Build a frequency table by hand for [3, 2, 3, 1, 2, 3], then reconstruct the sorted result.

Key point: Presence records uniqueness; frequency records multiplicity.

Source links: NIST: Counting sort
10. Q: How should counting sort handle a range that does not begin at zero?
A: Show answer
Let the minimum key be m and map each value v to index v − m. The number of possible keys is R = max − min + 1. When scanning the frequency structure, convert index i back to the original value m + i. This supports negative values as well as positive values, provided the range remains manageable. The same mapping technique applies to counting sort and bit vectors. Bounds should be checked before indexing, because negative Python indexes otherwise refer to elements from the end of a list rather than raising the intended domain error.

Study instruction: Map the values [−3, 0, −1, 2] to indexes using m = −3 and reconstruct their sorted order.

Key point: Offset indexing converts a bounded interval into zero-based indexes.

Source links: NIST: Counting sort
11. Q: Is the chapter's presence-based counting sort stable?
A: Show answer
No. Stability means that records with equal keys retain their relative input order. A presence array or frequency table that emits only key values does not retain the original positions of records, so it cannot by itself provide stable ordering of associated payloads. A stable counting sort typically computes cumulative counts and places each input record into a result array in a carefully chosen traversal order. Use the simple range-scan variant for integer keys when only sorted values are required; use a stable implementation when equal-key records carry information whose order must be preserved.

Study instruction: Create three records with the same key and different labels; explain what extra information and steps are needed to preserve their order.

Key point: Sorting keys and stably sorting records are different requirements.

Source links: NIST: Counting sort
12. Q: What is a Boolean array, and how can it represent a set?
A: Show answer
A Boolean array stores a True or False flag at each index. If each possible integer value has a corresponding index, True means the value belongs to the set and False means it does not. For example, setting indexes 1, 4, 5, and 9 to True represents the set {1, 4, 5, 9}. Membership and updates use direct indexing rather than hashing. The method is simple and can be fast, but its storage is determined by the full possible range, not by the number of members currently present.

Study instruction: Draw a Boolean array for the set {0, 2, 6} over the domain 0 through 7 and label each index.

Key point: An index represents a value; its Boolean flag represents membership.

Source links: Python truth-value testing; Python array module
13. Q: How does a Boolean array detect duplicates?
A: Show answer
Initialize all flags to False. For each input value, inspect its corresponding flag. If it is already True, the value has appeared before and the algorithm can return True. Otherwise set the flag to True and continue. Assuming the value-to-index mapping is valid, each membership check and update is constant time, so the scan is O(N), with O(R) storage for a range of R values. The implementation must validate bounds and account for any offset used to map the domain to indexes.

Study instruction: Trace the flags for [4, 2, 4] and identify the first point at which a duplicate is detected.

Key point: A previously set flag is direct evidence of a repeated value.

Source links: Python array module
14. Q: How can a Boolean array represent a range such as 3,000 through 4,000?
A: Show answer
Subtract the minimum value from each key before using it as an index. The mapping is index = value − 3000, and the reverse mapping is value = index + 3000. A domain with both endpoints included needs 1,001 flags. This avoids allocating indexes from zero through 4,000 when the relevant interval starts at 3,000. The same mapping technique applies to counting sort and bit vectors. Bounds should be checked before indexing, because negative Python indexes otherwise refer to elements from the end of a list rather than raising the intended domain error.

Study instruction: Convert values 3000, 3456, and 4000 into indexes and verify the reverse mapping.

Key point: Subtract the lower bound to obtain a compact zero-based domain.

Source links: Python sequence types
15. Q: Why can a Boolean array outperform a hash table even when both algorithms are O(N)?
A: Show answer
Direct indexing avoids computing a hash and resolving a hash-table slot for each lookup. It can also reduce indirection and improve locality for compact domains. These constant-factor effects can make the Boolean-array version faster in a particular benchmark. However, the advantage is not universal: initialization, range size, memory pressure, cache effects, interpreter behavior, and data distribution all matter. Benchmark representative inputs with repeated runs and compare both time and memory. Do not turn one set of measurements into a general performance guarantee.

Study instruction: Benchmark equivalent hash-set and Boolean-array implementations using the same generated inputs, and report the median across multiple runs.

Key point: Equal Big-O complexity does not imply equal practical runtime.

Source links: Python timeit
16. Q: What space trade-off appears when a set is used to speed up duplicate detection?
A: Show answer
A hash set grows with the number of distinct values stored, while a Boolean array reserves one flag for every possible value in the domain. Both consume auxiliary memory that the nested-loop method avoids. The hash set's storage is related to N, the number of stored values; the Boolean array's storage is related to R, the size of the domain. The appropriate choice depends on whether faster membership is worth the extra memory and whether the domain is compact enough to index directly.

Study instruction: For a workload with N = 100 and R = 10,000, estimate which representation is likely to reserve more logical slots and explain why.

Key point: Faster membership often requires an auxiliary representation.

Source links: Python sets; Python array module
17. Q: What is a bit vector?
A: Show answer
A bit vector represents a collection of Boolean membership flags as individual bits. A bit set to 1 means the corresponding value is present; a bit set to 0 means it is absent. The least significant, right-most bit conventionally represents index 0, with indexes increasing toward the left. Multiple flags are packed into integer storage, so a single integer can represent many possible values. Bit vectors are also called bit arrays, bit sets, bit strings, or bit maps. They are particularly useful for compact integer domains and for set operations that can be performed with bitwise operators.

Study instruction: Convert the set {0, 3, 4, 6} into an eight-bit pattern using index 0 as the least significant bit.

Key point: One bit stores one membership flag.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations; bitarray package
18. Q: Why does bit-vector indexing begin at the right-most bit?
A: Show answer
In positional binary notation, the right-most bit has weight 2⁰, the next bit has weight 2¹, and each position to the left doubles the weight. Treating the right-most bit as index 0 aligns the logical value index with the bit position used by arithmetic operations. Thus, a bit vector representing values 0, 3, 4, and 6 has ones at bit positions 0, 3, 4, and 6. This convention must be used consistently in masks, index calculations, conversion routines, and documentation; reversing the convention silently changes the represented set.

Study instruction: Label the bit indexes and decimal weights of the pattern 01011001 from right to left.

Key point: Bit index 0 is the least significant bit.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
19. Q: How can a Python integer be converted to and from a binary string?
A: Show answer
The built-in bin function returns a string with a 0b prefix followed by the binary digits; the prefix is notation, not part of the digit sequence. A binary literal such as 0b101101 is interpreted by Python as an integer. These facilities are useful for inspection and debugging, although converting to a string and scanning every character is not the most efficient way to read or modify a bit in an algorithm. For manual conversion exercises, validate each digit and accumulate the value by repeatedly multiplying by two and adding the next bit.

Study instruction: Verify that bin(37) represents 100101 and that the binary literal 0b100101 evaluates to 37.

Key point: bin converts an integer for display; a binary literal expresses an integer in source code.

Source links: Python bin function; Python integer literals
20. Q: What is the difference between a bit and a byte?
A: Show answer
A bit is one binary digit and can hold 0 or 1. A byte is eight bits. A group of eight bits can encode 256 distinct patterns, from 00000000 through 11111111. A bit vector uses individual bits as flags, while ordinary byte-oriented storage allocates at least one byte per stored unit. This is why bit packing can substantially reduce the logical storage needed for Boolean flags. Actual process memory also includes object headers, references, alignment, and container overhead, so logical bit counts should not be confused with total Python object size.

Study instruction: Calculate the number of bytes needed for 1,000, 8,000, and 32,000 raw bits, ignoring metadata and alignment.

Key point: Eight bits make one byte; object overhead is a separate cost.

Source links: Python bytes objects
21. Q: Does a Python integer always occupy exactly 32 bits?
A: Show answer
No. Python integers have arbitrary precision, subject to available memory, and are not restricted to a fixed 32-bit machine word. The chapter uses 32-bit chunks as an explanatory model for its array-backed bit vector. That is an implementation choice, not a guarantee about the size of a Python int object or the processor's native word size. A production implementation should document its chosen chunk width and use Python's actual integer behavior deliberately rather than assuming that the interpreter stores every integer in exactly four bytes.

Study instruction: Compare the bit lengths of 1, 2³¹, and 2¹⁰⁰ using int.bit_length(), and distinguish logical bit length from object size.

Key point: Python int is arbitrary precision; 32-bit chunks are a design convention here.

Source links: Python numeric types
22. Q: Why does an array-backed bit vector use several integers?
A: Show answer
A single fixed-width machine word can represent only as many flags as it has bits. To represent a larger domain, divide the domain into blocks and store one integer per block. In the chapter's 32-bit model, integer 0 represents values 0–31, integer 1 represents 32–63, integer 2 represents 64–95, and so on. Each added integer contributes another block of 32 logical flags. The approach keeps individual operations localized to one block while avoiding a separate Boolean slot for every possible value.

Study instruction: Determine which stored integer and local bit position represent values 31, 32, 64, and 95.

Key point: A block of B bits represents B consecutive logical values.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
23. Q: What are bitwise operations, and why are they central to bit vectors?
A: Show answer
Bitwise operations evaluate or transform corresponding binary positions of integer operands. AND, OR, and XOR combine two bit patterns; NOT inverts a pattern; shifts move bits left or right. These operations allow a program to inspect, set, clear, toggle, combine, or compare membership flags without converting the integer into a binary string. A bit vector is therefore more than a compact representation: its basic set operations can be implemented directly with machine-supported bitwise instructions or Python's integer operators.

Study instruction: Write the truth table for AND, OR, and XOR and predict the result of each operation before evaluating it in Python.

Key point: Bitwise operators manipulate the binary representation of integer values.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
24. Q: How does bitwise AND work?
A: Show answer
AND produces 1 at a position only when both operand bits are 1. Its truth table is 0 AND 0 = 0, 0 AND 1 = 0, 1 AND 0 = 0, and 1 AND 1 = 1. It is useful for testing whether a selected bit is set and for computing the intersection of two bit-vector sets. AND with 0 clears a bit; AND with 1 leaves the other bit unchanged. Those two properties make AND the basic operation for reading and clearing selected positions.

Study instruction: Convert 5 and 6 to binary, perform the AND by columns, and verify that the decimal result is 4.

Key point: AND keeps only positions that are 1 in both operands.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
25. Q: How does bitwise OR work?
A: Show answer
OR produces 1 at a position when at least one operand bit is 1. Its truth table is 0 OR 0 = 0, while 0 OR 1, 1 OR 0, and 1 OR 1 all produce 1. OR with 0 preserves the other bit, and OR with 1 sets the result to 1. In a bit vector, OR combines membership flags, so it implements set union: a value is present in the result if it is present in either input set.

Study instruction: Combine 10001001 and 01001100 using OR and list the set indexes represented by the result.

Key point: OR sets a position if either input has that position set.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
26. Q: How does bitwise XOR work, and what makes it useful?
A: Show answer
XOR, or exclusive OR, produces 1 when the two operand bits differ and 0 when they are equal. XOR with 0 leaves a bit unchanged; XOR with 1 flips it. Therefore XOR can toggle a selected bit, identify differing positions, and compute the sum of two binary operands before carry bits are added. It also has useful algebraic properties: a XOR a is zero, a XOR zero is a, and XOR is associative and commutative. These properties explain both the unique-unpaired-value puzzle and Hamming-distance calculation.

Study instruction: Evaluate 81 XOR 103 in binary and mark every position where the inputs differ.

Key point: XOR marks differences and cancels equal paired values.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
27. Q: What does Python's bitwise NOT operator do, and why can its decimal result look surprising?
A: Show answer
The ~ operator inverts the bits of an integer. Python integers are not fixed-width unsigned bit strings; they use an arbitrary-precision signed integer model whose bitwise behavior is defined as though negative values have an unbounded two's-complement sign extension. Consequently, ~x equals −x−1, so ~103 is −104 rather than the positive value one might expect from inverting only eight displayed bits. For a fixed-width bit pattern, first define the width and apply a mask to keep only the intended number of bits.

Study instruction: Verify that ~103 equals −104 and then mask the result to eight bits to inspect the low-byte pattern.

Key point: Python NOT is an unbounded signed-integer operation, not an eight-bit complement.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
28. Q: How do left and right shifts change a bit pattern?
A: Show answer
A left shift by k positions moves bits toward higher significance and fills the vacated low positions with zeros. For nonnegative integers, shifting left by k is equivalent to multiplying by 2ᵏ. A right shift moves bits toward lower significance and discards the low bits that fall off the right; for nonnegative integers, it is equivalent to floor division by 2ᵏ. Shift counts must be nonnegative. Python's right shift on negative integers is arithmetic and preserves the sign extension, so its behavior should not be confused with a fixed-width unsigned logical shift.

Study instruction: Evaluate 73 shifted left by 1 and 2, then 73 shifted right by 1; explain the discarded remainder.

Key point: Left shift scales by powers of two; right shift discards low-order bits.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
29. Q: Why does XOR calculate a binary sum when no carry is required?
A: Show answer
For one bit position, adding 0 and 0 gives 0, adding 0 and 1 gives 1, and adding 1 and 0 gives 1. XOR produces exactly those results. When both bits are 1, XOR produces 0, which is the correct low bit of the sum, but binary addition must also carry 1 into the next position. Therefore XOR computes the sum without carry. It is a partial addition operation, not a replacement for ordinary addition by itself.

Study instruction: Compare the binary addition of 5 and 2 with their XOR result, then repeat for 5 and 1 to expose the missing carry.

Key point: XOR gives the sum bits before carry propagation.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
30. Q: How can AND and left shift identify the carry bits in binary addition?
A: Show answer
A carry is generated at a bit position only when both operands contain 1 there. AND identifies exactly those positions. The carry must affect the next more significant position, so shifting the AND result left by one moves each generated carry to where it belongs. The expression (a AND b) shifted left by one therefore computes the carry number for that addition round. The XOR result supplies the sum without carry; the shifted AND result supplies the missing carry contribution.

Study instruction: For 5 + 1, calculate the XOR result and the shifted AND result separately, then add their binary values by hand.

Key point: AND detects carry generation; left shift moves each carry one position higher.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
31. Q: How does the iterative bitwise-addition algorithm finish an addition?
A: Show answer
Repeatedly compute sum_without_carry = a XOR b and carry = (a AND b) shifted left by one. Replace a with the sum_without_carry and b with the carry, then repeat. When the carry becomes zero, no further positions need carrying and a is the final sum. For nonnegative operands, carry propagation eventually stops. This is an educational demonstration of binary arithmetic; ordinary Python code should normally use the built-in + operator, which is clearer and handles the language's integer semantics directly.

Study instruction: Trace the loop for 7 + 1 and record a, b, the provisional sum, and carry at each iteration.

Key point: Repeat sum-without-carry and carry calculation until the carry is zero.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
32. Q: What is a bit mask?
A: Show answer
A bit mask is an integer pattern used with bitwise operators to isolate or modify selected positions in another integer. To inspect, set, or toggle one bit, a mask has a 1 at the target index and zeros elsewhere. To clear one bit, a mask has a 0 at the target and ones elsewhere. Masks allow a program to focus on one logical flag without converting the entire integer to a binary string. A common way to construct a single-bit mask is 1 shifted left by the desired index.

Study instruction: Construct masks for indexes 0, 4, and 7 and write their binary and decimal forms.

Key point: A mask selects which bit positions an operation can affect.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
33. Q: How can a function read one bit from an integer?
A: Show answer
Create a mask with a single 1 at the requested index, then AND it with the integer. If the result is nonzero, the target bit is 1; if the result is zero, it is 0. The mask is formed by shifting 1 left by the index. This is constant in the number of logical operations for a fixed-size word. A robust public function should reject negative indexes and, where a fixed width is intended, reject indexes beyond that width rather than silently reading an unbounded Python integer position.

Study instruction: Read indexes 2, 3, and 4 from integer 89 and compare the result with its binary representation.

Key point: AND with a one-hot mask reveals whether the selected bit is set.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
34. Q: How can a function set a bit to 1 without changing other bits?
A: Show answer
Build a one-hot mask and OR it with the integer. At the target position, OR with 1 always produces 1, regardless of the previous value. At every other position, OR with 0 preserves the original bit. Setting an already-set bit has no additional effect, so the operation is idempotent. In a mutable bit-vector class, assign the result back to the relevant stored word; Python integers themselves are immutable, so the operation creates a new integer value.

Study instruction: Set index 2 in 89 and then set it again; confirm that the second operation changes nothing.

Key point: OR with a one-hot mask sets one bit and preserves all others.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
35. Q: How can a function clear a bit to 0?
A: Show answer
Build a one-hot mask for the target index, invert it, and AND the inverted mask with the integer. The inverted mask contains 0 at the target and 1 everywhere else. AND therefore clears the target bit while preserving all other bits. In Python, the expression integer AND NOT (1 shifted left by index) works for nonnegative integers because the original integer has no set bits beyond its finite bit length. For fixed-width storage, explicitly constrain the result to the defined width when complementing masks or exposing the result as a bounded bit pattern.

Study instruction: Clear index 3 in 89, then clear it a second time and verify the result is unchanged.

Key point: AND with the inverse of a one-hot mask clears exactly one bit.

Source links: Python integer bitwise operations
36. Q: How can a function toggle a bit?
A: Show answer
Toggle means invert the selected bit: 0 becomes 1 and 1 becomes 0. Construct a one-hot mask and XOR it with the integer. XOR with 1 flips the target bit, while XOR with 0 preserves every other bit. Applying the same toggle twice restores the original value.
